Title: Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376

Published Time: Fri, 06 Nov 2026 03:41:50 GMT

Markdown Content:
\uselogo\reportnumber

Gil Tabak Affiliation: \thepa Clemens JS Schaefer Corresponding author: cjsschaefer@google.com Affiliation: \thepa

###### Abstract

Linear attention compresses context into a fixed-size key-value state for linear-time training and constant-memory inference. Gated delta-rule models decay this state and erase its prediction for an incoming key before writing a new value; Gated DeltaNet-2 (GDN-2) generalizes this with channel-wise decay and decoupled, full-rank erase and write gates. However, we show that state updates are far less sensitive to erasure than to writes: key activation and unit normalization concentrate key energy onto a sparse subset of channels, whereas value writes scale all channels, so the erase gate can be parameterized at lower rank. Furthermore, slow-decaying state rows accumulate much larger norms than fast rows, dominating unweighted readout and forcing stronger write-time erasure to suppress interference. Guided by this analysis, we introduce _Muir_, which fuses all gates into a sensitivity-allocated low-rank projection, re-weights state rows with a matched-filter readout gate, and reinvests the saved parameters in a wider feed-forward network. Because standard chunkwise algorithms become numerically unstable at the large chunk sizes needed to saturate wide matrix units, we design a parallel update for stable, efficient training. Across 166\text{M}\text{--}1.34\text{B} parameters, Muir lowers perplexity by 1.8\text{--}2.0\% over GDN-2 and 2.5\text{--}3.6\% over Transformer, raises commonsense accuracy by up to 1.9 points, and improves extractive recall over GDN-2. At 1.34\text{B}, Muir outperforms both baselines on multi-hop and multi-needle recall, extrapolates 2{,}560\times from its 4\text{K} training length to 10\text{M}-token streams without perplexity drift, and maintains near-100\% passkey retrieval to 1\text{M} tokens with static readout. Muir achieves these gains while matching GDN-2 throughput, reaching its 100\text{B}-token validation loss 1.2\times faster, and training 2.7\times faster than Transformer at 32\text{K}.

## 1 Introduction

As large language models scale to long-document comprehension, multi-hop reasoning, and in-context retrieval, the Transformer architecture ([Vaswani et al., 2017](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib116)) faces a fundamental scaling bottleneck: softmax self-attention requires training compute that grows quadratically with sequence length and an inference cache that grows linearly. Linear attention removes this bottleneck by compressing variable-length context into a fixed-size key-value state, enabling linear-time training and constant-memory inference ([Katharopoulos et al., 2020](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib60); [Choromanski et al., 2020](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib23); [Gu et al., 2021](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib40); [Orvieto et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib80); [Sun et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib113); [Gu and Dao, 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib39); [Yang et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib124); [Dao and Gu, 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib27); [De et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib29)). However, because a finite-dimensional state admits only finitely many orthogonal key directions, packing more keys into the state forces cross-key interference ([Johnson and Lindenstrauss, 1984](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib56); [Smolensky, 1990](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib108); [Arora et al., 2023a](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib3); [Jelassi et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib53); [Arora et al., 2024a](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib5)). Delta-rule recurrences ([Widrow and Hoff, 1960](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib120); [Schlag et al., 2021](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib101); [Yang et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib125); [Grazzi et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib38); [Siems et al., 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib107)) mitigate this interference by erasing the current prediction for an incoming key before writing a new value. In particular, Gated DeltaNet ([Yang et al., 2025b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib126)) pairs scalar exponential decay with delta-rule erasure, Kimi Linear ([Kimi Team et al., 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib61)) introduces channel-wise decay so state rows evolve at independent rates, and Gated DeltaNet-2 (GDN-2; [Hatamizadeh et al., 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib42)) generalizes both by decoupling erasure and writes into channel-wise gates so each state row and value channel updates independently.

![Image 1: Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376v1/figs/fig1_teaser.png)

Figure 1: Muir outperforms GDN-2 and Transformer on language modeling and long-context throughput while improving recall over GDN-2. (a) Sensitivity-allocated low-rank gates with FFN expansion drive the bulk of the validation NLL gain over GDN-2, while dynamic matched-filter readout drives the gain on extractive recall. (b) Across 166\text{M}\text{--}1.34\text{B} parameters, Muir lowers validation NLL by 18.0\text{--}20.4\text{ millinats} over GDN-2 and 25.0\text{--}36.2\text{ millinats} over Transformer. (c) On 8 TPU7x chips (0.5\text{M} tokens/step) at 1.34\text{B} with our chunkwise kernel, Muir matches GDN-2 throughput despite an additional readout gate and a 30.4\% wider FFN, and trains 1.64\times and 2.74\times faster than Transformer at 16\text{K} and 32\text{K}, respectively.

Parameterizing decay, erasure, and writes with channel-wise gates, however, introduces three bottlenecks across parameter allocation, state readout, and parallel training. First, when decoupling erasure and writes per channel, GDN-2 treats both gates symmetrically, allocating full-rank d\times d projections to each while compressing the decay and output gates through low-rank bottlenecks; these two full-rank matrices alone consume 2d^{2} parameters (one-sixth of the 12d^{2} layer budget), so the gates together shrink the feed-forward network (FFN) width by over 25\% to remain iso-parameter with a Transformer. Second, because state rows decay at different rates, slow-decaying rows accumulate value writes over hundreds to thousands of tokens while fast-decaying rows retain only recent inputs, so state row norms span more than an order of magnitude. Under unweighted query readout, high-norm slow rows dominate the output and force stronger write-time erasure to suppress their interference, which prematurely overwrites stored associations. Third, because channels decay at independent rates, chunkwise parallel training ([Sun et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib113); [Yang et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib124); [Yang et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib125); [Yang et al., 2025b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib126)) must divide keys by cumulative decay prefixes to compute within-chunk interactions via matrix multiplication and solve a lower-triangular system per chunk, which breaks down on wide-matrix-unit accelerators. While GPU kernels ([Yang and Zhang, 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib123); [Kimi Team et al., 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib61)) avoid full-chunk division and solve diagonal triangular sub-blocks sequentially in SRAM at small chunks matched to 16\times 16 TensorCores, accelerators that pair wide systolic matrix units with a much slower vector unit (such as AWS Trainium and Google TPUs, reaching 256\times 256 matrix units with vector units over 60\times slower on modern TPUs; [Austin et al., 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib7); [Jiang et al., 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib54)) require large chunks for high matrix utilization. At these chunk sizes, cumulative decay prefixes underflow on fast-decaying channels and overflow the division; alternatively, materializing pairwise channel decays or solving the triangular system sequentially falls back to the slow vector unit.

To address these challenges, we analyze channel-wise gated delta recurrences and show that state updates are far less sensitive to erasure than to writes: rectifying key activations (such as \mathrm{SiLU}; [Ramachandran et al., 2018](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib95)) and unit normalization concentrate key energy onto a sparse subset of channels, whereas value writes scale all channels, so the erase gate requires a much lower-rank projection than the write gate. We further derive a closed-form scaling law for state row norms and show that weighting queries by a matched-filter readout gain balances row contributions, which lowers the write-time erasure needed to suppress interference and extends the memory horizon. Guided by this analysis, we introduce _Muir_, a gated delta-rule model that fuses all recurrence and output gates into a sensitivity-allocated low-rank projection, modulates queries with a matched-filter readout gate (combining a static channel profile with dynamic per-token modulation) to re-weight state rows, and reinvests the saved gate parameters under a fixed budget to widen the FFN and expand its capacity for factual associations ([Geva et al., 2021](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib36); [Meng et al., 2022](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib72)). Finally, to train stably at the large chunk sizes needed to saturate wide matrix units, we design a boundary-pivoted chunkwise kernel that splits relative decays without division or full-chunk 3D decay tensors and solves within-chunk triangular systems via block matrix multiplications.

[Figure 1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S1.F1 "In 1 Introduction ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") summarizes our main findings. Across model scales from 166\text{M} to 1.34\text{B} parameters, Muir outperforms both GDN-2 and Transformer on language modeling and commonsense reasoning, and improves over GDN-2 on real-world extractive recall ([Arora et al., 2024a](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib5); [Arora et al., 2024b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib6)). At 1.34\text{B}, Muir improves synthetic multi-hop and multi-needle retrieval ([Hsieh et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib49)) over both baselines, and extrapolates from 4\text{K} to 10\text{M}-token streams in a 25\text{ MB}float32 state without perplexity drift, whereas Dynamic YaRN Transformer ([Peng et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib85)) diverges past 8\text{K} and needs a 2.1\text{ TB}bfloat16 KV cache at 10\text{M}. On passkey retrieval, our static matched-filter readout achieves near-100\% accuracy to 1\text{M} tokens, whereas GDN-2 collapses past 4\text{K}\text{--}8\text{K}. Using our chunkwise kernel for both recurrent models and Splash Attention (TPU implementation of FlashAttention; [Dao et al., 2022](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib28); [Dao, 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib26)) for Transformer, Muir matches GDN-2 training throughput across sequence lengths despite a wider FFN and an additional gate, reaches the 100\text{B}-token validation loss of GDN-2 1.2\times faster, and trains 2.7\times faster than Transformer at 32\text{K}. Mechanistic analysis confirms that state updates are more sensitive to writes than to erasure; state row norms closely follow our scaling law; and the learned readout balances state rows, lowers write-time erasure, and retains associations over longer contexts.

## 2 Preliminaries

##### Notation.

Throughout the paper, scalars and scalar entries of vectors or matrices are denoted by lightface letters (m,T,{v}_{j},{M}_{i,j}), vectors by boldface lowercase letters ({\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{m}}), and matrices by boldface uppercase letters ({\bm{M}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}). All vectors are row vectors, so the Euclidean inner product of {\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{m}} is \langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\rangle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\bm{u}}{\bm{v}}^{\top} and the rank-one outer product of {\bm{u}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{m}} and {\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{n}} is {\bm{u}}^{\top}{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}. Additionally, for vectors {\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{m}},{\bm{x}}\odot{\bm{y}},{\bm{x}}\oslash{\bm{y}}, and {\bm{x}}^{\odot p} denote elementwise multiplication, division, and p-th power, respectively, and \Diag({\bm{x}})\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m} is the diagonal matrix with diagonal {\bm{x}}. For a matrix {\bm{M}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},{\bm{M}}_{i,:} and {\bm{M}}_{:,j} denote its i-th row and j-th column, and \tril({\bm{M}}),\stril({\bm{M}}), and \triu({\bm{M}}) denote its lower-triangular, strictly lower-triangular, and upper-triangular parts. We denote the Kronecker delta by \delta_{ij} (\delta_{ij}=1 if i=j and 0 otherwise). For a differentiable map f,\mathrm{D}_{{\bm{x}}}f({\bm{x}})[\delta{\bm{x}}]\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lim_{{\epsilon}\to 0}{\epsilon}^{-1}(f({\bm{x}}+{\epsilon}\,\delta{\bm{x}})-f({\bm{x}})) denotes the directional derivative at {\bm{x}} along perturbation \delta{\bm{x}}. Finally, a hat (\hat{\cdot}) denotes gate-modulated quantities (e.g., \hat{\bm{k}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\bm{b}}\odot{\bm{k}}), a bar (\bar{\cdot}) denotes expectations or empirical averages, and an asterisk (∗) denotes stationary fixed points or optimal solutions. (See [Appendix B](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A2 "Appendix B Summary of Notation ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") for a summary of notation.)

### 2.1 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Rule

Given an input sequence {\bm{x}}_{1},\dotsc,{\bm{x}}_{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{d}}, linear-time sequence models avoid the {\mathcal{O}}(T^{2}) training cost and {\mathcal{O}}(T) key-value cache of softmax attention by compressing the token history into a fixed-size associative memory {\bm{S}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{v}} per head across N_{h} heads of dimension d_{k}=d_{v}=d/N_{h} (typically d_{k}=d_{v}=128). Following standard practice in modern linear attention models, where local feature mixing before state updates is critical for in-context recall ([Gu and Dao, 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib39); [Arora et al., 2023a](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib3); [Yang et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib125); [Allen-Zhu, 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib1)), each token {\bm{x}}_{t} is projected by {\bm{W}}_{q},{\bm{W}}_{k},{\bm{W}}_{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}, passed through a depthwise causal convolution and \mathrm{SiLU} activation, and partitioned into N_{h} heads. Within each head, queries and keys are \ell_{2}-normalized to the unit sphere ({\bm{q}}_{t},{\bm{k}}_{t}\in\mathbb{S}^{d_{k}-1}, so \|{\bm{q}}_{t}\|_{2}=\|{\bm{k}}_{t}\|_{2}=1; [Schlag et al., 2021](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib101); [Yang et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib125)) for bounded addressing and erasure stability, whereas values {\bm{v}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{d_{v}}} enter additively and remain unnormalized to preserve feature magnitude. Standard linear attention accumulates outer products additively ({\bm{S}}_{t}={\bm{S}}_{t-1}+{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}{\bm{v}}_{t}; [Katharopoulos et al., 2020](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib60)) or with exponential decay ({\bm{S}}_{t}={\alpha}_{t}{\bm{S}}_{t-1}+{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}{\bm{v}}_{t} for scalar {\alpha}_{t}([Sun et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib113); [Dao and Gu, 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib27)); or \Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t-1}+{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}{\bm{v}}_{t} for channel-wise {\bm{\alpha}}_{t}([Yang et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib124))). By contrast, the delta rule ([Widrow and Hoff, 1960](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib120); [Schlag et al., 2021](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib101); [Yang et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib125)) updates {\bm{S}}_{t} via one step of online gradient descent on the instantaneous key-value reconstruction loss \frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{k}}_{t}{\bm{S}}-{\bm{v}}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}, querying the state along {\bm{k}}_{t} and erasing its current prediction before writing the new value (see [Appendix A](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A1 "Appendix A Additional Related Work ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") for additional related work).

##### Gated DeltaNet-2.

GDN-2 ([Hatamizadeh et al., 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib42)) extends scalar-decay Gated DeltaNet ([Yang et al., 2025b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib126)) by promoting the per-head decay factor and erase step size to channel-wise vectors alongside a channel-wise write gate ([Appendix C](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A3 "Appendix C Online Regression Formulation ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") derives this update via a left preconditioner \Diag({\bm{b}}_{t})^{-1} on the gated reconstruction loss). Specifically, at timestep t, each head computes four channel-wise gates from {\bm{x}}_{t}: a decay (retention) gate {\bm{\alpha}}_{t}\in(0,1)^{1\times d_{k}}, an erase gate {\bm{b}}_{t}\in(0,2)^{1\times d_{k}}, a write gate {\bm{w}}_{t}\in(0,1)^{1\times d_{v}}, and a post-recurrence output gate {\bm{g}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{d_{v}}}. Given pre-activation logits {\bm{z}}^{f}_{t},{\bm{z}}^{b}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{d_{k}}} and {\bm{z}}^{w}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{d_{v}}}, the recurrence gates are parameterized as \log{\bm{\alpha}}_{t}=-\exp(a_{\log})\,\mathrm{softplus}({\bm{z}}^{f}_{t}),{\bm{b}}_{t}=2\sigma({\bm{z}}^{b}_{t}), and {\bm{w}}_{t}=\sigma({\bm{z}}^{w}_{t}), where {\bm{z}}^{f}_{t} includes a bias {\bm{c}}_{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{d_{k}}} and a_{\log}\in\mathbb{R} is a per-head log-rate.

With the decayed prior state \hat{\bm{S}}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t-1} (whose j-th row \hat{\bm{S}}_{t,j,:}={\alpha}_{t,j}{\bm{S}}_{t-1,j,:} scales key channel j\in\{1,\dotsc,d_{k}\}), the erase-gated key \hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\bm{b}}_{t}\odot{\bm{k}}_{t}, and the write-gated value \hat{\bm{v}}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\bm{w}}_{t}\odot{\bm{v}}_{t}, the GDN-2 state update and readout are given by

{\bm{S}}_{t}=\big(\mathbf{I}-{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}({\bm{b}}_{t}\odot{\bm{k}}_{t})\big)\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t-1}+{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}({\bm{w}}_{t}\odot{\bm{v}}_{t})=\hat{\bm{S}}_{t}-{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}(\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\hat{\bm{S}}_{t})+{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{v}}_{t},\qquad{\bm{o}}_{t}={\bm{q}}_{t}{\bm{S}}_{t}.(1)

Here, \ell_{2}-normalization of {\bm{k}}_{t} ensures that \langle{\bm{k}}_{t},{\bm{b}}_{t}\odot{\bm{k}}_{t}\rangle\in(0,2) for any {\bm{b}}_{t}\in(0,2)^{1\times d_{k}}, so the erasure factor \mathbf{I}-{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}({\bm{b}}_{t}\odot{\bm{k}}_{t})_contracts_ the state along {\bm{k}}_{t} with eigenvalue 1-\langle{\bm{k}}_{t},{\bm{b}}_{t}\odot{\bm{k}}_{t}\rangle\in(-1,1)([Grazzi et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib38); [Hatamizadeh et al., 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib42)). The head outputs {\bm{o}}_{t} are normalized via per-head \mathrm{RMSNorm}, modulated elementwise by \mathrm{SiLU}({\bm{g}}_{t})([Sun et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib113); [Yang et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib124); [Qiu et al., 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib92)), concatenated across the N_{h} heads, and projected back to the residual stream by {\bm{W}}_{o}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}.

##### Gate Parameterization and Parameter Budget.

A language model stacks L residual layers, each pairing the sequence-mixing block above with a feed-forward network \mathrm{FFN}({\bm{y}})=\mathrm{GELU}({\bm{y}}{\bm{W}}_{\mathrm{in}}){\bm{W}}_{\mathrm{out}}([Hendrycks and Gimpel, 2016](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib46)) of intermediate width d_{\mathrm{ffn}} ({\bm{W}}_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{\mathrm{ffn}}},{\bm{W}}_{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{ffn}}\times d}). A standard Transformer layer with projections {\bm{W}}_{q},{\bm{W}}_{k},{\bm{W}}_{v},{\bm{W}}_{o}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} (4d^{2} parameters) and FFN width d_{\mathrm{ffn}}=4d (2d\,d_{\mathrm{ffn}}=8d^{2} parameters) contains 12d^{2} non-embedding parameters. Across all N_{h} heads, GDN-2 parameterizes the decay logit {\bm{z}}^{f}_{t} and output gate {\bm{g}}_{t} with rank-r_{0} bottleneck projections ({\bm{W}}_{f\downarrow},{\bm{W}}_{g\downarrow}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r_{0}} and {\bm{W}}_{f\uparrow},{\bm{W}}_{g\uparrow}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{0}\times d} with r_{0}=128, totaling 4dr_{0}=512d parameters), whereas the erase logit {\bm{z}}^{b}_{t}={\bm{x}}_{t}{\bm{W}}_{b} and write logit {\bm{z}}^{w}_{t}={\bm{x}}_{t}{\bm{W}}_{w} use unbiased full-rank (dense) matrices {\bm{W}}_{b},{\bm{W}}_{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} totaling 2d^{2} parameters (one-sixth of the 12d^{2} layer budget). Following standard practice of adjusting d_{\mathrm{ffn}} to maintain a 12d^{2} per-layer parameter budget for iso-parameter comparisons ([Shazeer, 2020](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib105); [Sun et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib113); [Qiu et al., 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib92); [Hatamizadeh et al., 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib42)), GDN-2 sets d_{\mathrm{ffn}}=3d-2r_{0}=3d-256 (shrinking d_{\mathrm{ffn}} from 8{,}192 to 5{,}888 at d=2{,}048).

### 2.2 Chunkwise Parallel Form

Evaluating ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ Gated DeltaNet-2. ‣ 2.1 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Rule ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) sequentially over T timesteps performs {\mathcal{O}}(d_{k}d_{v}) vector operations per token per head, which has low arithmetic intensity and is memory-bandwidth-bound on modern hardware. Defining the _effective write_\tilde{\bm{v}}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hat{\bm{v}}_{t}-\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\hat{\bm{S}}_{t}=\hat{\bm{v}}_{t}-\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{d_{v}}} (gated memory write minus gated memory read) rewrites ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ Gated DeltaNet-2. ‣ 2.1 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Rule ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) in the Gated Linear Attention (GLA; [Yang et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib124)) form:

{\bm{S}}_{t}=\big(\mathbf{I}-{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\big)\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t-1}+{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{v}}_{t}=\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t-1}+{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}\big(\hat{\bm{v}}_{t}-\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t-1}\big)=\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t-1}+{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{v}}_{t}.

To expose matrix-matrix multiplications during training, chunkwise-parallel algorithms ([Sun et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib113); [Yang et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib124); [Yang et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib125); [Yang et al., 2025b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib126)) partition the sequence into N_{c}=T/C chunks of length C. Consider a single chunk indexed by t\in\{1,\dotsc,C\} starting from chunk-initial state {\bm{S}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{v}}. Let {\bm{\pi}}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\prod_{r=1}^{t}{\bm{\alpha}}_{r} and {\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\prod_{r=s+1}^{t}{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}\in(0,1]^{1\times d_{k}} (with {\bm{\pi}}_{t,t}={\bm{1}}) denote the elementwise cumulative decay prefixes and relative decays; unrolling {\bm{S}}_{t-1} from {\bm{S}}_{0} in \tilde{\bm{v}}_{t}=\hat{\bm{v}}_{t}-\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t-1} and using \hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\Diag({\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}){\bm{k}}_{s}^{\top}=(\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}){\bm{k}}_{s}^{\top}=\langle\hat{\bm{k}}_{t},{\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}\odot{\bm{k}}_{s}\rangle gives

\tilde{\bm{v}}_{t}=\hat{\bm{v}}_{t}-({\bm{\pi}}_{t}\odot\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{0}-\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\langle\hat{\bm{k}}_{t},{\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}\odot{\bm{k}}_{s}\rangle\tilde{\bm{v}}_{s},\qquad t\in\{1,\dotsc,C\}.

Stacking \tilde{\bm{v}}_{t},\hat{\bm{v}}_{t},{\bm{\pi}}_{t}\odot\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}, and {\bm{k}}_{t}\oslash{\bm{\pi}}_{t} across t\in\{1,\dotsc,C\} as the rows of \tilde{\bm{V}},\hat{\bm{V}}\in\mathbb{R}^{C\times d_{v}} and \hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi},{\bm{K}}_{\oslash\pi}\in\mathbb{R}^{C\times d_{k}} casts this coupled recurrence in matrix form as

\tilde{\bm{V}}=\hat{\bm{V}}-\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}{\bm{S}}_{0}-\stril\!\big(\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}{\bm{K}}_{\oslash\pi}^{\top}\big)\tilde{\bm{V}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad{\bm{T}}\tilde{\bm{V}}=\hat{\bm{V}}-\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}{\bm{S}}_{0},\qquad{\bm{T}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbf{I}+{\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}}\in\mathbb{R}^{C\times C},

where {\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}} is strictly lower-triangular with entries ({\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}})_{t,s}=\langle\hat{\bm{k}}_{t},{\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}\odot{\bm{k}}_{s}\rangle (t>s). Solving {\bm{T}}\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{U}}&{\bm{Y}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{V}}&\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}\end{bmatrix} yields \tilde{\bm{V}}={\bm{T}}^{-1}\hat{\bm{V}}-({\bm{T}}^{-1}\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}){\bm{S}}_{0}={\bm{U}}-{\bm{Y}}{\bm{S}}_{0}. Once \tilde{\bm{V}} is known, unrolling the GLA recurrence {\bm{S}}_{t}=\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t-1}+{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{v}}_{t} across the chunk ([Yang et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib124)) gives the end-of-chunk state {\bm{S}}_{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{v}} (passed as {\bm{S}}_{0} to the next chunk) and chunk outputs {\bm{O}}\in\mathbb{R}^{C\times d_{v}} via matrix multiplications:

{\bm{S}}_{C}=\Diag({\bm{\pi}}_{C}){\bm{S}}_{0}+\tilde{\bm{K}}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{V}},\qquad{\bm{O}}={\bm{Q}}_{\odot\pi}{\bm{S}}_{0}+{\bm{M}}\tilde{\bm{V}},

where \tilde{\bm{K}},{\bm{Q}}_{\odot\pi}\in\mathbb{R}^{C\times d_{k}} have rows {\bm{\pi}}_{C,s}\odot{\bm{k}}_{s} and {\bm{\pi}}_{t}\odot{\bm{q}}_{t}, and {\bm{M}}\in\mathbb{R}^{C\times C} is lower-triangular with entries {M}_{t,s}=\langle{\bm{q}}_{t},{\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}\odot{\bm{k}}_{s}\rangle for t\geq s.

In Gated DeltaNet ([Yang et al., 2025b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib126)), where {\bm{\alpha}}_{t}={\alpha}_{t}{\bm{1}} and {\bm{b}}_{t}={\beta}_{t}{\bm{1}}, scalar multiplication commutes with rank-one erasure: {\alpha}_{t}(\mathbf{I}-{\beta}_{t}{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}{\bm{k}}_{t})=(\mathbf{I}-{\beta}_{t}{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}{\bm{k}}_{t}){\alpha}_{t}, so the scalar decay ratio {\pi}_{t}/{\pi}_{s} factors out of the inner product \langle\hat{\bm{k}}_{t},{\bm{k}}_{s}\rangle. Under channel-wise decay {\bm{\alpha}}_{t} in ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ Gated DeltaNet-2. ‣ 2.1 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Rule ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), however, the diagonal decay operator \Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}) and the rank-one erasure operator \mathbf{I}-{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{k}}_{t} do not commute whenever {\bm{\alpha}}_{t} varies across channels:

\big(\mathbf{I}-{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\big)\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t})\neq\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t})\big(\mathbf{I}-{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\big).

This non-commutativity introduces two obstacles to executing the chunkwise solve on wide-matrix-unit accelerators. First, forming {\bm{T}} and {\bm{M}} faces a tradeoff between efficiency and numerical stability: factoring {\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}}=\stril(\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}{\bm{K}}_{\oslash\pi}^{\top}) and {\bm{M}}=\tril({\bm{Q}}_{\odot\pi}{\bm{K}}_{\oslash\pi}^{\top}) as matrix multiplications divides keys by {\bm{\pi}}_{s}, which underflows bfloat16 and float32 (minimum positive normal \approx 1.18\times 10^{-38}) over long chunks (e.g., {\pi}_{s,j}\leq 2^{-256}\approx 10^{-77} at C=256 whenever {\alpha}_{r,j}\leq 0.5) and overflows {\bm{K}}_{\oslash\pi}. Conversely, evaluating {\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}=\exp({\bm{\Lambda}}_{t}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{s}) from the cumulative log-decay matrix {\bm{\Lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}^{C\times d_{k}} (with rows {\bm{\Lambda}}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{r=1}^{t}\log{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}\leq{\bm{0}}) is numerically stable for t\geq s, but materializing the 3D tensor {\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}\odot{\bm{k}}_{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{C\times C\times d_{k}} (32\text{ MB} per head in float32 at C=256) and reducing over d_{k} executes on the vector unit. Second, once {\bm{T}}=\mathbf{I}+{\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}} is formed, solving {\bm{T}}\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{U}}&{\bm{Y}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{V}}&\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}\end{bmatrix} via forward substitution is sequential across the C steps of the chunk and also executes on the vector unit.

##### Wide-Matrix-Unit Architecture and Tiling.

We study wide-matrix-unit execution on Google TPU7x Ironwood accelerators ([Jiang et al., 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib54)), where each chip comprises two TensorCores totaling 192\text{ GB} of High-Bandwidth Memory (HBM) at 7{,}380\text{ GB/s} and 128\text{ MB} of on-chip Vector Memory (VMEM). Within each TensorCore, two Matrix Multiply Units (MXUs) operate on 256\times 256 systolic arrays (bfloat16 inputs accumulated in float32, 2{,}307\text{ TFLOP/s} per chip), whereas the Vector Processing Unit (VPU, a 128\text{-lane}\times 8\text{-sublane} SIMD processor) has roughly 64\times lower bfloat16 throughput (\approx\!36\text{ TFLOP/s} in bfloat16 and \approx\!18\text{ TFLOP/s} in float32 per chip; [Austin et al., 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib7)). However, when either dimension of a matrix multiplication is smaller than 256, the MXU pads unused lanes with zeros, reducing MXU utilization proportionally. Across HBM, VMEM, and Vector Registers (VREG), XLA tiles the last two dimensions of every tensor into 2D tiles of shape T(8, 128) for 32-bit words (T(16, 128) in packed bfloat16), matching the 128 VPU lanes; elementwise operations that preserve the trailing dimensions fuse in VREGs, whereas permutations that alter the tiled dimensions incur an HBM round-trip.

## 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics

In this section, we analyze how the channel-wise gates ({\bm{\alpha}}_{t},{\bm{b}}_{t},{\bm{w}}_{t}) and unweighted readout {\bm{o}}_{t}={\bm{q}}_{t}{\bm{S}}_{t} in the GDN-2 recurrence govern state updates and associative retrieval. In §[3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.SS1 "3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), we study this recurrence from single-step updates to long-horizon stationary dynamics: we first quantify the single-step sensitivity of {\bm{S}}_{t} to perturbations in {\bm{b}}_{t} and {\bm{w}}_{t}, next characterize how channel-wise decay and erasure determine the expected squared norms of the state rows over long sequences, and finally analyze how weighting state rows at readout affects retrieval error and interacts with write-time erasure. We then test our theoretical predictions in controlled synthetic memory experiments in §[3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.SS2 "3.2 Synthetic Memory Experiments ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"). (All proofs are in [Appendix D](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A4 "Appendix D Mathematical Proofs ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout").)

### 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics

In GDN-2, both the erase and write gates are parameterized by dense projections, yet they enter the state update asymmetrically: {\bm{b}}_{t} modulates the \mathrm{SiLU}-activated, unit-norm key {\bm{k}}_{t} against the decayed prior state \hat{\bm{S}}_{t}, whereas {\bm{w}}_{t} scales the unnormalized value {\bm{v}}_{t}. We first formalize the resulting asymmetry in state sensitivity.

###### Proposition 3.1(State sensitivity to erase and write gates).

Consider the GDN-2 state update ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ Gated DeltaNet-2. ‣ 2.1 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Rule ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) at step t as a function {\bm{S}}_{t}({\bm{b}}_{t},{\bm{w}}_{t}) of the erase gate {\bm{b}}_{t} and write gate {\bm{w}}_{t}, with decayed prior state \hat{\bm{S}}_{t}=\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t-1}.

1.   (i)For any perturbations \delta{\bm{b}} and \delta{\bm{w}}, the directional derivatives of {\bm{S}}_{t} with respect to {\bm{b}}_{t} and {\bm{w}}_{t} are

\mathrm{D}_{{\bm{b}}}{\bm{S}}_{t}[\delta{\bm{b}}]=-{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}\big((\delta{\bm{b}}\odot{\bm{k}}_{t})\hat{\bm{S}}_{t}\big),\qquad\mathrm{D}_{{\bm{w}}}{\bm{S}}_{t}[\delta{\bm{w}}]={\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}(\delta{\bm{w}}\odot{\bm{v}}_{t}).(2) 
2.   (ii)Conditioned on ({\bm{k}}_{t},{\bm{v}}_{t},\hat{\bm{S}}_{t}), the expected squared Frobenius sensitivities under isotropic perturbations \delta{\bm{b}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},\mathbf{I}_{d_{k}}) and \delta{\bm{w}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},\mathbf{I}_{d_{v}}) are

\mathbb{E}_{\delta{\bm{b}}}\!\left[\|\mathrm{D}_{{\bm{b}}}{\bm{S}}_{t}[\delta{\bm{b}}]\|_{F}^{2}\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{k}_{t,j}^{2}\|\hat{\bm{S}}_{t,j,:}\|_{2}^{2},\qquad\mathbb{E}_{\delta{\bm{w}}}\!\left[\|\mathrm{D}_{{\bm{w}}}{\bm{S}}_{t}[\delta{\bm{w}}]\|_{F}^{2}\right]=\|{\bm{v}}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}.(3) 
3.   (iii)Decompose the gated key \hat{\bm{k}}_{t}={\bm{b}}_{t}\odot{\bm{k}}_{t} into its parallel erase strength {\mu}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\langle{\bm{k}}_{t},\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\rangle along {\bm{k}}_{t} and its orthogonal residual {\bm{s}}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}-{\mu}_{t}{\bm{k}}_{t} (\langle{\bm{s}}_{t},{\bm{k}}_{t}\rangle=0), whose squared Euclidean norm equals the {\bm{k}}_{t}^{\odot 2}-weighted within-head coordinate variance of {\bm{b}}_{t} given by

\|{\bm{s}}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}-{\mu}_{t}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{k}_{t,j}^{2}\big({b}_{t,j}-{\mu}_{t}\big)^{2}.

Moreover, the gradient \nabla_{{\bm{b}}}{\mu}_{t}={\bm{k}}_{t}^{\odot 2}=({k}_{t,1}^{2},\dotsc,{k}_{t,d_{k}}^{2}) has squared Euclidean norm

\|\nabla_{{\bm{b}}}{\mu}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}=\|{\bm{k}}_{t}\|_{4}^{4}=\frac{1}{\eta_{\mathrm{eff}}({\bm{k}}_{t})}\in\left[\frac{1}{d_{k}},1\right],\quad\text{where}\quad\eta_{\mathrm{eff}}({\bm{k}}_{t})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\big(\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{k}_{t,j}^{2}\big)^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{k}_{t,j}^{4}}=\frac{1}{\|{\bm{k}}_{t}\|_{4}^{4}}\in[1,d_{k}]

is the per-token key participation ratio. 

Having shown that single-step state updates are far less sensitive to {\bm{b}}_{t} than to {\bm{w}}_{t}, we now analyze how channel-wise decay and erasure shape the expected energies \mathbb{E}[\|{\bm{S}}_{t,j,:}\|_{2}^{2}] of the state rows {\bm{S}}_{t,j,:} over long sequences. For the rest of this section, let ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ Gated DeltaNet-2. ‣ 2.1 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Rule ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) be driven by a stationary i.i.d. stream ({\bm{k}}_{t},\hat{\bm{v}}_{t},{\bm{\alpha}}_{t},{\bm{b}}_{t}) whose components are mutually independent at each timestep t (and hence independent of {\bm{S}}_{t-1}). Additionally, let \mathbb{E}[\hat{\bm{v}}_{t}]={\bm{0}} and \mathbb{E}[\|\hat{\bm{v}}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}]={\sigma}_{v}^{2}, keys {\bm{k}}_{t} have uncorrelated channels \mathbb{E}[{k}_{t,i}{k}_{t,j}]=\delta_{ij}{c}_{j} with second moments {c}_{j}>0 (\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{c}_{j}=1), and the gates have mean squared decay factor {\lambda}_{j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{E}[{\alpha}_{t,j}^{2}]\in(0,1) and mean channel erase rate \bar{b}_{j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{E}[{b}_{t,j}]\in(0,2) satisfying \bar{b}_{j}{c}_{j}<1.

###### Proposition 3.4(Stationary row-energy law).

Let {m}_{t,j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{E}[\|{\bm{S}}_{t,j,:}\|_{2}^{2}] denote the expected energy of state row j\in\{1,\dotsc,d_{k}\} at timestep t. Under the stationary stream above, the uncorrelated-row condition \mathbb{E}[\langle{\bm{S}}_{t-1,i,:},{\bm{S}}_{t-1,j,:}\rangle]=\delta_{ij}{m}_{t-1,j}, and the second-order closures \mathbb{E}[{k}_{t,i}^{2}{k}_{t,j}^{2}]\approx{c}_{i}{c}_{j} and \mathbb{E}[{b}_{t,i}^{2}]\approx\bar{b}_{i}^{2}, the row energy {m}_{t,j} of ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ Gated DeltaNet-2. ‣ 2.1 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Rule ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) evolves according to the linear recurrence

{m}_{t,j}={\lambda}_{j}(1-2\bar{b}_{j}{c}_{j})\,{m}_{t-1,j}+{c}_{j}\sum_{i=1}^{d_{k}}\bar{b}_{i}^{2}{c}_{i}{\lambda}_{i}\,{m}_{t-1,i}+{c}_{j}{\sigma}_{v}^{2}.(4)

Because the cross-row sum \sum_{i=1}^{d_{k}}\bar{b}_{i}^{2}{c}_{i}{\lambda}_{i}{m}_{t-1,i} is a scalar shared across all rows j (rescaling {\sigma}_{v}^{2} by a constant factor without altering relative row energies), dropping this cross-row coupling term yields the unique stationary row energy {m}_{j}^{\ast}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lim_{t\to\infty}{m}_{t,j} and stationary row root-mean-square (RMS) norm {\rho}_{j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sqrt{\smash{{m}_{j}^{\ast}}\vphantom{{m}}} in closed form:

{m}_{j}^{\ast}={\rho}_{j}^{2}=\frac{{c}_{j}{\sigma}_{v}^{2}}{1-{\lambda}_{j}+2{\lambda}_{j}\bar{b}_{j}{c}_{j}}={c}_{j}{\sigma}_{v}^{2}\,\tau_{\mathrm{eff},j}(\bar{b}_{j}),\qquad\tau_{\mathrm{eff},j}(\bar{b}_{j})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{1}{1-{\lambda}_{j}+2{\lambda}_{j}\bar{b}_{j}{c}_{j}}.(5)

Under the standard (unweighted) readout {\bm{o}}_{t}={\bm{q}}_{t}{\bm{S}}_{t}, rows with large stationary RMS {\rho}_{j} dominate the inner product with {\bm{q}}_{t} and mask low-energy, fast-decaying channels. To characterize how a per-channel query gain {\bm{\gamma}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{d_{k}}} remedies this imbalance, consider an associative retrieval setting in which a key-value pair ({\bm{k}}_{t-\ell},\hat{\bm{v}}_{t-\ell}) was written at timestep t-\ell and the model queries {\bm{S}}_{t} at timestep t along {\bm{q}}_{t}={\bm{k}}_{t-\ell} to recover \hat{\bm{v}}_{t-\ell}; we refer to the elapsed timestep count \ell\geq 1 as the _retrieval lag_.

###### Proposition 3.6(Lag-dependent matched-filter readout).

Suppose at timestep t, the state row j\in\{1,\dotsc,d_{k}\} decomposes at retrieval lag \ell\geq 1 as {\bm{S}}_{t,j,:}={a}_{\ell,j}{k}_{t-\ell,j}\hat{\bm{v}}_{t-\ell}+{\bm{\xi}}_{t,j}, where {a}_{\ell,j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\prod_{s=t-\ell+1}^{t}{\alpha}_{s,j}(1-{b}_{s,j}{k}_{s,j}^{2}) is the cumulative diagonal retention over the \ell intervening timesteps (with off-diagonal cross-row key products centered in {\bm{\xi}}_{t,j} under \mathbb{E}[{k}_{s,i}{k}_{s,j}]=\delta_{ij}{c}_{j}) and {\bm{\xi}}_{t,j} is zero-mean multi-write interference from timesteps s\neq t-\ell, uncorrelated with \hat{\bm{v}}_{t-\ell} and satisfying \mathbb{E}[\langle{\bm{\xi}}_{t,i},{\bm{\xi}}_{t,j}\rangle\mid{\bm{k}}_{t-\ell}]=\delta_{ij}{m}_{j}^{\ast}. Assume {a}_{\ell,j} is independent of ({\bm{k}}_{t-\ell},\hat{\bm{v}}_{t-\ell}) with mean \bar{a}_{\ell,j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{E}[{a}_{\ell,j}]\in(0,1) and small gate variance \mathbb{E}[{a}_{\ell,i}{a}_{\ell,j}]\approx\bar{a}_{\ell,i}\bar{a}_{\ell,j} (so \bar{a}_{\ell,j}\approx{\lambda}_{j}^{\ell\mkern-0.5mu\smash{\raisebox{0.55pt}{\scalebox{0.78}{$\scriptstyle/$}}}\mkern-0.5mu2} when \bar{b}_{j}{c}_{j}\ll 1). For a query {\bm{q}}_{t}={\bm{k}}_{t-\ell} and gain-modulated readout {\bm{o}}_{t}({\bm{\gamma}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}({\bm{\gamma}}\odot{\bm{q}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t}, the following hold:

1.   (i)Under the second-order closure \mathbb{E}[{k}_{t-\ell,i}^{2}{k}_{t-\ell,j}^{2}]\approx{c}_{i}{c}_{j}, the expected retrieval mean-squared error (MSE) {\mathcal{L}}_{\ell}({\bm{\gamma}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{E}[\|{\bm{o}}_{t}({\bm{\gamma}})-\hat{\bm{v}}_{t-\ell}\|_{2}^{2}] is

{\mathcal{L}}_{\ell}({\bm{\gamma}})={\sigma}_{v}^{2}\!\left(1-\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{\gamma}_{j}{c}_{j}\bar{a}_{\ell,j}\right)^{\!2}+\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{\gamma}_{j}^{2}{c}_{j}{m}_{j}^{\ast}.(6) 
2.   (ii)The unit-signal interference variance \sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{\gamma}_{j}^{2}{c}_{j}{m}_{j}^{\ast} (subject to \sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{\gamma}_{j}{c}_{j}\bar{a}_{\ell,j}=1) and the unconstrained MSE {\mathcal{L}}_{\ell}({\bm{\gamma}}) are both uniquely minimized along the matched-filter direction {\gamma}_{j}\propto\bar{a}_{\ell,j}/{m}_{j}^{\ast}, with unconstrained minimizer {\gamma}_{j}^{\ast}(\ell) (\kappa_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\sigma}_{v}^{2}/\big(1+{\sigma}_{v}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d_{k}}{c}_{i}\bar{a}_{\ell,i}^{2}/{m}_{i}^{\ast}\big)):

{\gamma}_{j}^{\ast}(\ell)=\kappa_{\ell}\,\frac{\bar{a}_{\ell,j}}{{m}_{j}^{\ast}}\propto\frac{\bar{a}_{\ell,j}}{{\rho}_{j}^{2}}\approx\frac{{\lambda}_{j}^{\ell\mkern-0.5mu\smash{\raisebox{0.55pt}{\scalebox{0.78}{$\scriptstyle/$}}}\mkern-0.5mu2}}{{\rho}_{j}^{2}},\qquad j\in\{1,\dotsc,d_{k}\}.(7) 

When a head is restricted to a static gain vector {\bm{\gamma}} shared across timesteps, marginalizing ([6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.E6 "Equation 6 ‣ Item 0(i) ‣ Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) over a distribution of retrieval lags characterizes the optimal time-invariant compromise.

###### Corollary 3.8(Horizon-marginalized static gain and fractional power law).

Suppose a static gain {\bm{\gamma}} minimizes the interference variance in ([6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.E6 "Equation 6 ‣ Item 0(i) ‣ Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) subject to the horizon-averaged signal constraint \mathbb{E}_{\ell}\big[\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{\gamma}_{j}{c}_{j}\bar{a}_{\ell,j}\big]=1 under a geometric lag distribution of mean horizon H>1, with \bar{a}_{\ell,j}={\lambda}_{j}^{\ell\mkern-0.5mu\smash{\raisebox{0.55pt}{\scalebox{0.78}{$\scriptstyle/$}}}\mkern-0.5mu2}. Replacing \bar{a}_{\ell,j} in ([7](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.E7 "Equation 7 ‣ Item 0(ii) ‣ Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) by the horizon-averaged signal survival g_{H}({\lambda}_{j})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{E}_{\ell}[{\lambda}_{j}^{\ell\mkern-0.5mu\smash{\raisebox{0.55pt}{\scalebox{0.78}{$\scriptstyle/$}}}\mkern-0.5mu2}] yields the static matched filter

{\gamma}_{j}^{\mathrm{stat}}\propto\frac{g_{H}({\lambda}_{j})}{{m}_{j}^{\ast}}=\frac{g_{H}({\lambda}_{j})}{{\rho}_{j}^{2}},\qquad g_{H}({\lambda}_{j})=\frac{\sqrt{{\lambda}_{j}}}{1+(H-1)(1-\sqrt{{\lambda}_{j}})},\qquad j\in\{1,\dotsc,d_{k}\}.

In the decay-dominated regime (2{\lambda}_{j}\bar{b}_{j}{c}_{j}\ll 1-{\lambda}_{j}\ll 1 at fixed {c}_{j}), where {m}_{j}^{\ast}={\rho}_{j}^{2}\approx{c}_{j}{\sigma}_{v}^{2}\tau_{\mathrm{eff},j} with \tau_{\mathrm{eff},j}=(1-{\lambda}_{j})^{-1}, the local log-slope of {\gamma}_{j}^{\mathrm{stat}} with respect to the row RMS {\rho}_{j}\propto\tau_{\mathrm{eff},j}^{1\mkern-0.5mu\smash{\raisebox{0.55pt}{\scalebox{0.78}{$\scriptstyle/$}}}\mkern-0.5mu2} satisfies

-\frac{\mathrm{d}\log{\gamma}_{j}^{\mathrm{stat}}}{\mathrm{d}\log{\rho}_{j}}\approx\frac{4\tau_{\mathrm{eff},j}}{2\tau_{\mathrm{eff},j}+H-1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}p(\tau_{\mathrm{eff},j})\in(0,2),

which satisfies {\gamma}_{j}^{\mathrm{stat}}\propto{\rho}_{j}^{-p} with an intermediate exponent p\in(0,1) (strictly between unit gain p=0 and full inverse-RMS whitening p=1) for all channels with \tau_{\mathrm{eff},j}<(H-1)/2.

Finally, we connect query-time readout back to write-time erasure. Consider a head with channel-uniform mean erase rate \bar{b}_{j}=\bar{b}\in(0,2) in ([5](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.E5 "Equation 5 ‣ Proposition 3.4 (Stationary row-energy law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) (where {b}_{t,j}=\bar{b} implies {\mu}_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{b}_{t,j}{k}_{t,j}^{2}=\bar{b}). Choosing \bar{b} faces a fundamental tension: increasing \bar{b} suppresses multi-write interference by contracting every row’s effective memory horizon \tau_{\mathrm{eff},j}(\bar{b}) in ([5](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.E5 "Equation 5 ‣ Proposition 3.4 (Stationary row-energy law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), but simultaneously attenuates stored target associations by a factor of 1-\bar{b}{c}_{j} on each intervening write step ([Proposition 3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). Because matched-filter readout already damps interference from high-energy rows at query time, it relaxes the erasure required at write time.

###### Proposition 3.10(Erase-readout substitution).

Consider a head with channel-uniform mean erase rate \bar{b}_{j}=\bar{b}\in(0,2) and horizon-averaged signal survival g_{H}({\lambda}_{j}). For any readout gain {\bm{\gamma}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{d_{k}}} with expected horizon-averaged signal gain S({\bm{\gamma}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{\gamma}_{j}{c}_{j}g_{H}({\lambda}_{j})>0, define the normalized interference-to-signal ratio:

{\mathcal{I}}(\bar{b},{\bm{\gamma}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{\gamma}_{j}^{2}{c}_{j}{m}_{j}^{\ast}(\bar{b})}{{\sigma}_{v}^{2}\,S({\bm{\gamma}})^{2}}=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}\frac{{\gamma}_{j}^{2}{c}_{j}^{2}}{S({\bm{\gamma}})^{2}\big(1-{\lambda}_{j}+2{\lambda}_{j}\bar{b}{c}_{j}\big)}=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}\frac{{\gamma}_{j}^{2}{c}_{j}^{2}}{S({\bm{\gamma}})^{2}}\,\tau_{\mathrm{eff},j}(\bar{b}).

Let \bar{\tau}(\bar{b})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathcal{I}}(\bar{b},{\bm{1}}) denote the baseline interference ratio under unit readout ({\bm{\gamma}}={\bm{1}}), and let B\colon(0,2)\to\mathbb{R} be a twice-differentiable erasure cost (capturing target-signal attenuation and, for \bar{b}>1, over-relaxation error) satisfying B^{\prime}(\bar{b})>0 and B^{\prime\prime}(\bar{b})\geq 0. For any readout interference multiplier \Phi>0 relative to \bar{\tau}(\bar{b}), define the equilibrium erase rate:

\bar{b}^{\ast}(\Phi)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\argmin_{\bar{b}\in(0,2)}\;{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{b};\Phi),\qquad{\mathcal{J}}(\bar{b};\Phi)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Phi\,\bar{\tau}(\bar{b})+B(\bar{b}).

Then, at any \bar{b}\in(0,2) for which the channel noise-to-signal ratios {m}_{j}^{\ast}(\bar{b})/g_{H}({\lambda}_{j}) vary across j, the static matched filter {\bm{\gamma}}^{\ast} satisfying S({\bm{\gamma}}^{\ast})=S({\bm{1}}) achieves a strict interference reduction \Phi^{\ast}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathcal{I}}(\bar{b},{\bm{\gamma}}^{\ast})/\bar{\tau}(\bar{b})<1, and any interior minimizer \bar{b}^{\ast}(\Phi) satisfies

\bar{b}^{\ast}(\Phi^{\ast})<\bar{b}^{\ast}(1),\qquad\tau_{\mathrm{eff},j}\!\big(\bar{b}^{\ast}(\Phi^{\ast})\big)>\tau_{\mathrm{eff},j}\!\big(\bar{b}^{\ast}(1)\big)\quad\forall\,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d_{k}\}.

### 3.2 Synthetic Memory Experiments

To test our theoretical predictions under controlled data-generating processes that satisfy our theoretical assumptions, we design two synthetic associative-memory tasks: a _single-head stationary retrieval task_ that isolates [Propositions 3.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition 3.4 (Stationary row-energy law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), [3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[3.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem10 "Proposition 3.10 (Erase-readout substitution). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and [Corollary 3.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem8 "Corollary 3.8 (Horizon-marginalized static gain and fractional power law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), and an _end-to-end learned multi-query associative recall task_ that tests [Propositions 3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition 3.1 (State sensitivity to erase and write gates). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), [3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[3.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem10 "Proposition 3.10 (Erase-readout substitution). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") under gradient descent. (Additional details in [Appendix E](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A5 "Appendix E Synthetic Memory Experiment Details ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout").)

#### 3.2.1 Synthetic Task Setup

##### Single-Head Stationary Retrieval.

In this task, we simulate a single gated-delta head (d_{k}=d_{v}=64) driven by a stationary stream of unit-norm keys {\bm{k}}_{t}\in\mathbb{S}^{d_{k}-1} with anisotropic power-law channel second moments {c}_{j}=\mathbb{E}[{k}_{t,j}^{2}]>0 (\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{c}_{j}=1) and isotropic values {\bm{v}}_{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}({\bm{0}},d_{v}^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{d_{v}}) ({\sigma}_{v}^{2}=1). To isolate stationary row-energy accumulation and query readout from learned gate projections, we fix the write gate to {\bm{w}}_{t}={\bm{1}} (so \hat{\bm{v}}_{t}={\bm{v}}_{t}), the erase gate to a uniform scalar {\bm{b}}_{t}={\mu}{\bm{1}} with {\mu}=\bar{b}\in(0,2) (so {\mu}_{t}\equiv{\mu} and {\bm{s}}_{t}\equiv{\bm{0}}), and the decay vector {\bm{\alpha}}\in(0,1)^{1\times d_{k}} to time-invariant factors with log-spaced timescales \tau_{j} ({\alpha}_{j}=e^{-1\mkern-0.5mu\smash{\raisebox{0.55pt}{\scalebox{0.78}{$\scriptstyle/$}}}\mkern-0.5mu\tau_{j}},{\lambda}_{j}={\alpha}_{j}^{2}). At each timestep t, the state {\bm{S}}_{t} updates via ([1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ Gated DeltaNet-2. ‣ 2.1 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Rule ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), and we sample a retrieval lag \ell_{t}\sim p(\ell) and query {\bm{S}}_{t} along the historical key {\bm{q}}_{t}={\bm{k}}_{t-\ell_{t}} with readout gain {\bm{\gamma}} to recover {\bm{v}}_{t-\ell_{t}} under normalized MSE {\mathcal{L}}({\bm{\gamma}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\sigma}_{v}^{-2}\mathbb{E}[\|{\bm{o}}_{t}({\bm{\gamma}})-{\bm{v}}_{t-\ell_{t}}\|_{2}^{2}]:

{\bm{S}}_{t}=\big(\mathbf{I}-{\mu}{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}{\bm{k}}_{t}\big)\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}){\bm{S}}_{t-1}+{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}{\bm{v}}_{t},\qquad{\bm{o}}_{t}({\bm{\gamma}})=\big({\bm{\gamma}}\odot{\bm{k}}_{t-\ell_{t}}\big){\bm{S}}_{t}.

We consider two lag distributions p(\ell). First, under a _geometric lag horizon_ of mean H, we sweep the erase strength {\mu}\in(0,2) across the stability interval of the rank-one erasure contraction \mathbf{I}-{\mu}{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}{\bm{k}}_{t} and compare five static readout profiles (each scaled by a least-squares scalar prefactor \kappa^{\ast} fitted on a disjoint split, corresponding to \kappa_{\ell} in ([7](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.E7 "Equation 7 ‣ Item 0(ii) ‣ Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"))): unit gain {\gamma}_{j}=1 (p=0; GDN-2), half-power scaling {\gamma}_{j}\propto 1/\sqrt{\smash{{\rho}_{j}}\vphantom{{\rho}}} (p=1/2), full inverse-RMS whitening {\gamma}_{j}\propto 1/{\rho}_{j} (p=1), the [Corollary 3.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem8 "Corollary 3.8 (Horizon-marginalized static gain and fractional power law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") horizon filter {\gamma}_{j}^{\mathrm{stat}}\propto g_{H}({\lambda}_{j})/{m}_{j}^{\ast}, and the unconstrained d_{k}-parameter least-squares static oracle {\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{LS}}. Second, under a _bimodal two-lag mixture_ p(\ell)=\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{\ell,2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{\ell,80} at the unit-gain optimal erase strength {\mu}={\mu}_{\mathrm{unit}}^{\ast}, we compare the shared d_{k}-parameter static oracle {\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{LS}} against two lag-dependent readouts: the [Proposition 3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") analytic filter {\gamma}_{j}^{\ast}(\ell)\propto{\lambda}_{j}^{\ell\mkern-0.5mu\smash{\raisebox{0.55pt}{\scalebox{0.78}{$\scriptstyle/$}}}\mkern-0.5mu2}/{m}_{j}^{\ast} (one scalar prefactor per lag) and the lag-conditioned least-squares oracle {\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{LS}}(\ell).

##### Learned Multi-Query Associative Recall.

We train a single-layer, two-head model (N_{h}=2,d_{k}=d_{v}=32,d=128) end-to-end on _streaming_ multi-query associative recall ([Arora et al., 2023a](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib3)). Each sequence spans T=256 write/read events over K=256 keys and V=64 values, activating K_{\mathrm{live}}=96 keys (1.5\times the total state rank N_{h}d_{k}=64) with \zipf(1.0) frequencies. Each timestep t\in\{1,\dotsc,T\} is a write (prob. 0.7) binding an active key to a fresh value in \{1,\dotsc,V\} (overwriting prior bindings), or a read (prob. 0.3) querying a written key for its latest value. To ensure the write gate ({\bm{w}}_{t}\odot{\bm{v}}_{t}) must dynamically suppress inactive value channels, we partition values across four orthogonal subspaces of \mathbb{R}^{1\times{d}} and inject isotropic Gaussian noise into the inactive subspaces. From RMS-normalized {\bm{x}}_{t}, each head h\in\{1,2\} computes unit-norm \mathrm{SiLU} queries and keys ({\bm{q}}_{t}^{\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle($}}}h\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}} and {\bm{k}}_{t}^{\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle($}}}h\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}), values {\bm{v}}_{t}^{\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle($}}}h\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}, and rank-8 decay gates {\bm{\alpha}}_{t}^{\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle($}}}h\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}, projecting concatenated head outputs to V-way read logits.

We vary two architectural axes: (i) to test [Proposition 3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition 3.1 (State sensitivity to erase and write gates). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), we parameterize the erase gate {\bm{b}}_{t} and write gate {\bm{w}}_{t} across heads either with a dense projection {\bm{x}}_{t}{\bm{W}}_{m} (m\in\{b,w\}, full rank N_{h}d_{k}=64) or a rank-r_{m} bottleneck {\bm{x}}_{t}{\bm{W}}_{m\downarrow}{\bm{W}}_{m\uparrow}+{\bm{c}}_{m}, sweeping r_{m}\in\{1,2,4,8,16,32\} for one gate while keeping the other dense; and (ii) to test [Propositions 3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[3.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem10 "Proposition 3.10 (Erase-readout substitution). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), we compare unit ({\bm{\gamma}}_{t}={\bm{1}}), static ({\bm{\gamma}}_{t}=2\sigma({\bm{c}}_{q})), and rank-8 dynamic ({\bm{\gamma}}_{t}=2\sigma({\bm{x}}_{t}{\bm{W}}_{q\downarrow}{\bm{W}}_{q\uparrow}+{\bm{c}}_{q})) readout gains (partitioned across heads into {\bm{b}}_{t}^{\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle($}}}h\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}},{\bm{w}}_{t}^{\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle($}}}h\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}},{\bm{\gamma}}_{t}^{\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle($}}}h\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}):

\displaystyle{\bm{b}}_{t}=2\sigma({\bm{x}}_{t}{\bm{W}}_{b\downarrow}{\bm{W}}_{b\uparrow}+{\bm{c}}_{b}),\qquad{\bm{w}}_{t}=\sigma({\bm{x}}_{t}{\bm{W}}_{w\downarrow}{\bm{W}}_{w\uparrow}+{\bm{c}}_{w}),\qquad{\bm{\gamma}}_{t}=2\sigma({\bm{x}}_{t}{\bm{W}}_{q\downarrow}{\bm{W}}_{q\uparrow}+{\bm{c}}_{q});
\displaystyle{\bm{o}}_{t}^{\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle($}}}h\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}=\big({\bm{\gamma}}_{t}^{\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle($}}}h\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}\odot{\bm{q}}_{t}^{\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle($}}}h\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}\big){\bm{S}}_{t}^{\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle($}}}h\smash{\raisebox{0.48pt}{\scalebox{0.80}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}.

![Image 2: Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376v1/figs/fig2_toys.png)

Figure 2: Matched-filter readout and low-rank erase gates improve synthetic associative retrieval and lower optimal erase strength. (a) Full whitening worsens retrieval error relative to unit gain (GDN-2), whereas half-power scaling and the [Corollary 3.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem8 "Corollary 3.8 (Horizon-marginalized static gain and fractional power law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") filter reduce error and shift {\mu}^{\ast} from 0.60 to 0.50, with the [Corollary 3.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem8 "Corollary 3.8 (Horizon-marginalized static gain and fractional power law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") filter matching the static oracle. (b) On a 50/50 mixture of lags \ell\in\{2,80\} at {\mu}=0.60, the lag-matched oracle outperforms the static oracle across all horizons, while the [Proposition 3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") filter matches the lag-matched oracle at \ell=80 and beats the static oracle on long and mixed horizons. (c) Low-rank erase gates r_{b} outperform dense gates at every bottleneck rank on long-lag recall, whereas low-rank write gates r_{w} degrade below dense at r_{w}\leq 8 and recover once r_{w}\geq 16.

#### 3.2.2 Synthetic Task Results

[Figure 2](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.F2 "In Learned Multi-Query Associative Recall. ‣ 3.2.1 Synthetic Task Setup ‣ 3.2 Synthetic Memory Experiments ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") summarizes the results across both tasks over 20 seeds (full tables in [Appendix E](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A5 "Appendix E Synthetic Memory Experiment Details ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). In the single-head stationary setting at {\mu}={\mu}_{\mathrm{unit}}^{\ast}=0.60, the closed-form law ([5](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.E5 "Equation 5 ‣ Proposition 3.4 (Stationary row-energy law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) ([Proposition 3.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition 3.4 (Stationary row-energy law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) matches the empirical row energies {m}_{j} across a 52.4\times dynamic range with Spearman and log-Pearson correlations of 1.0. Sweeping {\mu}\in(0,2) under the geometric lag horizon of mean H=27 (Figure [2 a](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ Learned Multi-Query Associative Recall. ‣ 3.2.1 Synthetic Task Setup ‣ 3.2 Synthetic Memory Experiments ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), full inverse-RMS whitening 1/{\rho}_{j} (p=1) worsens retrieval MSE relative to unit gain because dividing by {\rho}_{j} amplifies fast-decaying rows whose target signal has vanished ([Remark 3.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem9 "Remark 3.9 (Suboptimality of full inverse-RMS whitening). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). By contrast, half-power scaling 1/\sqrt{\smash{{\rho}_{j}}\vphantom{{\rho}}} improves over unit gain and the [Corollary 3.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem8 "Corollary 3.8 (Horizon-marginalized static gain and fractional power law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") horizon filter {\gamma}_{j}^{\mathrm{stat}}\propto g_{H}({\lambda}_{j})/{m}_{j}^{\ast} matches the 64-parameter static oracle {\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{LS}}, with both filters shifting the optimal erase strength from {\mu}^{\ast}=0.60 down to 0.50 ([Proposition 3.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem10 "Proposition 3.10 (Erase-readout substitution). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). On the bimodal \ell_{t}\in\{2,80\} mixture (Figure [2 b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ Learned Multi-Query Associative Recall. ‣ 3.2.1 Synthetic Task Setup ‣ 3.2 Synthetic Memory Experiments ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), the lag-conditioned oracle {\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{LS}}(\ell) correlates negatively with the channel decay factor {\alpha}_{j} at \ell=2 (Spearman r_{s}=-0.98) and reverses sign at \ell=80 (r_{s}=+0.83), in accordance with [Remark 3.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem7 "Remark 3.7 (Incompatibility of static gain across retrieval lags). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"). Consequently, lag-matched readout {\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{LS}}(\ell) (64 parameters per lag) outperforms the static oracle {\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{LS}} (64 parameters) across all horizons. Meanwhile, the [Proposition 3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") filter, fitting just one scalar per lag, matches {\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{LS}}(\ell) at \ell=80 and outperforms the static oracle on long and mixed horizons.

In the learned associative recall task (Figure [2 c](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ Learned Multi-Query Associative Recall. ‣ 3.2.1 Synthetic Task Setup ‣ 3.2 Synthetic Memory Experiments ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), sweeping the bottleneck rank for {\bm{b}}_{t} vs. {\bm{w}}_{t} confirms [Proposition 3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition 3.1 (State sensitivity to erase and write gates). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"): compressing {\bm{b}}_{t} (keeping {\bm{w}}_{t} dense) improves long-lag recall over dense GDN-2 at every rank while cutting erase-logit token variance on writes (3.0\%\text{--}15.6\% vs. 27.9\%; [Remark 3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem3 "Remark 3.3 (Regularization of oblique erase variance by low-rank projections). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). Conversely, compressing {\bm{w}}_{t} (with {\bm{b}}_{t} dense) to r_{w}\leq 8 degrades long-lag recall below the dense baseline, whereas r_{w}=16 approaches dense recall and r_{w}=32 surpasses it. Finally, when both gates are compressed to (r_{b},r_{w})=(4,4), replacing unit readout ({\bm{\gamma}}_{t}={\bm{1}}) with static {\bm{\gamma}} and dynamic {\bm{\gamma}}_{t} monotonically raises long-lag recall (+4.18 points over unit readout, +2.00 over dense gates) while lowering final-timestep state energy \mathbb{E}[\|{\bm{S}}_{T}\|_{F}^{2}] by 45.7\% compared to unit readout (64.3\% vs. dense gates), matching [Propositions 3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[3.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem10 "Proposition 3.10 (Erase-readout substitution). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout").

## 4 Muir

![Image 3: Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376v1/figs/fig3_architecture.png)

Figure 3: Muir co-designs asymmetric low-rank gate parameterization and matched-filter query readout with MXU tile alignment and iso-parameter FFN reallocation. (a) Standard residual block with a Muir token mixer. (b) Within the Muir block, {\bm{x}}_{t} feeds linear-\mathrm{Conv}-\mathrm{SiLU} pathways for ({\bm{q}}_{t},{\bm{k}}_{t},{\bm{v}}_{t}) alongside a single fused low-rank gate projection that produces all five gates (\log{\bm{\alpha}}_{t},{\bm{b}}_{t},{\bm{g}}_{t},{\bm{w}}_{t},{\bm{\gamma}}_{t}). (c) Asymmetric gate ranks (r_{f},r_{b},r_{g},r_{w},r_{q})=(64,64,128,128,128) sum to R_{\mathrm{tot}}=512=2\times 256, which fills two 256-column MXU tiles in the fused down-projection and aligns (f,b) and (g,w,q) on 128-lane VMEM tile boundaries for batched up-projections. (d) At d=2{,}048, replacing the dense (b,w) gate matrices in GDN-2 cuts gate-projection parameters by 4.5\times; this either reduces per-layer parameters and FLOPs by 14.6\% at fixed FFN width, or frees +7.3\text{M} parameters per layer to widen the FFN under an iso-parameter budget.

In this section, we translate our theoretical findings in §[3](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3 "3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") into the Muir architecture ([Figure 3](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.F3 "In 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) and its TPU-efficient chunkwise training algorithm ([Figure 4](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.F4 "In Custom Backward Pass. ‣ 4.2 Boundary-Pivoted Chunkwise Kernel ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")).1 1 1 We name _Muir_ (pronounced _mewr_) after Muir Woods National Monument and its ancient coastal redwoods, whose rings preserve centuries of history, echoing long-horizon state memory. Following [Propositions 3.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition 3.4 (Stationary row-energy law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), [3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), [3.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem7 "Remark 3.7 (Incompatibility of static gain across retrieval lags). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[3.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem10 "Proposition 3.10 (Erase-readout substitution). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), each Muir head augments the four channel-wise gates of GDN-2 ({\bm{\alpha}}_{t},{\bm{b}}_{t},{\bm{w}}_{t},{\bm{g}}_{t}) with a fifth channel-wise gate, a dynamic query readout gain {\bm{\gamma}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{d_{k}}} that scales the \ell_{2}-normalized query prior to state readout. With the gain-modulated query \hat{\bm{q}}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\bm{\gamma}}_{t}\odot{\bm{q}}_{t} alongside \hat{\bm{S}}_{t}=\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t-1},\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}={\bm{b}}_{t}\odot{\bm{k}}_{t}, and \hat{\bm{v}}_{t}={\bm{w}}_{t}\odot{\bm{v}}_{t}, the Muir state update and readout are

{\bm{S}}_{t}=\big(\mathbf{I}-{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}({\bm{b}}_{t}\odot{\bm{k}}_{t})\big)\Diag({\bm{\alpha}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t-1}+{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}({\bm{w}}_{t}\odot{\bm{v}}_{t})=\hat{\bm{S}}_{t}-{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}\big(\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\hat{\bm{S}}_{t}\big)+{\bm{k}}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{v}}_{t},\qquad{\bm{o}}_{t}=\hat{\bm{q}}_{t}{\bm{S}}_{t}=({\bm{\gamma}}_{t}\odot{\bm{q}}_{t}){\bm{S}}_{t},(8)

followed by per-head \mathrm{RMSNorm}({\bm{o}}_{t})\odot\mathrm{SiLU}({\bm{g}}_{t}) and the output projection {\bm{W}}_{o}.

### 4.1 Low-Rank Gating and Matched-Filter Readout

While GDN-2 already parameterizes the decay and output gates (f,g) in low rank, it uses dense d\times d matrices for the erase and write gates (b,w) to provide full channel-wise flexibility. However, [Propositions 3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition 3.1 (State sensitivity to erase and write gates). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem3 "Remark 3.3 (Regularization of oblique erase variance by low-rank projections). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") establish that a low-rank bottleneck on {\bm{b}}_{t} suffices to preserve the channel-wise erase control of GDN-2 along the active key subspace while projecting out the oblique erase variance \|{\bm{s}}_{t}\|_{2}^{2} that inflates the state-transition spectral norm above 1, whereas the write gate {\bm{w}}_{t} modulates all d_{v} value channels directly and requires higher projection rank than {\bm{b}}_{t}. We therefore replace the separate low-rank (f,g) and dense (b,w) projections in GDN-2 with a single _fused_ low-rank gate projection covering all five gates: decay f, erase gate b, output gate g, write gate w, and query readout gain q.
