Given the token representation {\bm{x}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{d}}, we compute a single fused down-projection {\bm{W}}_{\downarrow}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times R_{\mathrm{tot}}} into a bottleneck of total rank R_{\mathrm{tot}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}r_{f}+r_{b}+r_{g}+r_{w}+r_{q}, followed by five per-gate up-projections {\bm{W}}_{m\uparrow}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{m}\times d} with learned channel biases {\bm{c}}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{d}}:

{\bm{h}}_{t}={\bm{x}}_{t}{\bm{W}}_{\downarrow}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{R_{\mathrm{tot}}}},\quad\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{h}}^{f}_{t}&{\bm{h}}^{b}_{t}&{\bm{h}}^{g}_{t}&{\bm{h}}^{w}_{t}&{\bm{h}}^{q}_{t}\end{bmatrix}={\bm{h}}_{t},\quad{\bm{z}}^{m}_{t}={\bm{h}}^{m}_{t}{\bm{W}}_{m\uparrow}+{\bm{c}}_{m}\;\;\text{for }m\in\{f,b,g,w,q\}.(9)

After partitioning each {\bm{z}}^{m}_{t} across the N_{h} heads, the five gate vectors in ([8](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.E8 "Equation 8 ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) are parameterized as

\log{\bm{\alpha}}_{t}=-\exp(a_{\log})\,\mathrm{softplus}({\bm{z}}^{f}_{t}),\quad{\bm{b}}_{t}=2\sigma({\bm{z}}^{b}_{t}),\quad{\bm{w}}_{t}=\sigma({\bm{z}}^{w}_{t}),\quad{\bm{g}}_{t}={\bm{z}}^{g}_{t},\quad{\bm{\gamma}}_{t}={\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{stat}}\odot 2\sigma({\bm{z}}^{q}_{t}).

Unlike the _unbiased_ dense (b,w) projections in GDN-2, including a bias {\bm{c}}_{m} on every up-projection gives each channel an independent operating point despite the low-rank bottleneck ({\bm{c}}_{f},{\bm{c}}_{g} are inherited from GDN-2, while {\bm{c}}_{b},{\bm{c}}_{w},{\bm{c}}_{q} are newly added in Muir).

For the readout gain {\bm{\gamma}}_{t}, we initialize {\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{stat}}={\bm{1}} and {\bm{W}}_{q\uparrow}={\bm{0}} (with {\bm{c}}_{q}={\bm{0}}) so that 2\sigma({\bm{z}}^{q}_{t})={\bm{1}} and {\bm{\gamma}}_{t}={\bm{1}} at initialization. Scaling the sigmoid into (0,2) centers the dynamic multiplier at 1 so each query can either attenuate (<\!1) or amplify (>\!1) state rows relative to {\gamma}_{j}^{\mathrm{stat}} in {\bm{o}}_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{d_{k}}{\gamma}_{t,j}{q}_{t,j}{\bm{S}}_{t,j,:}, whereas an unscaled sigmoid in (0,1) could only damp {\gamma}_{j}^{\mathrm{stat}}. This factored form lets {\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{stat}} learn the horizon-marginalized power-law profile ([Corollary 3.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem8 "Corollary 3.8 (Horizon-marginalized static gain and fractional power law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) and 2\sigma({\bm{z}}^{q}_{t}) learn the lag-dependent matched filter ([Proposition 3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), while scale-invariant \mathrm{RMSNorm}({\bm{o}}_{t}) absorbs the global factor \kappa_{\ell} in ([7](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.E7 "Equation 7 ‣ Item 0(ii) ‣ Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). Unlike learned per-channel query-key scaling ([Hua et al., 2022](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib51); [Dehghani et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib30); [Lahoti et al., 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib63)), {\bm{\gamma}}_{t} scales only {\bm{q}}_{t} at readout so {\bm{k}}_{t} remains unit-norm (\|{\bm{k}}_{t}\|_{2}=1) for contractive erasure.

##### Rank Allocation and Hardware Alignment.

We set the bottleneck ranks (r_{f},r_{b},r_{g},r_{w},r_{q}) in ([9](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.E9 "Equation 9 ‣ 4.1 Low-Rank Gating and Matched-Filter Readout ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) to satisfy two constraints: (i) motivated by the sensitivity asymmetry in [Proposition 3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Proposition 3.1 (State sensitivity to erase and write gates). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), the gates that modulate full-dimensional vectors directly (output gate g, write gate w, and query readout gain q) receive twice the rank of the gates governing state decay and key-contracted erasure (decay f and erase gate b), and (ii) the total rank R_{\mathrm{tot}} is an exact multiple of the 256-column TPU7x MXU tile width. Setting (r_{f},r_{b},r_{g},r_{w},r_{q})=(64,64,128,128,128) gives R_{\mathrm{tot}}=512=2\times 256.2 2 2 In ablations that omit the token-dependent factor 2\sigma({\bm{z}}^{q}_{t}) (r_{q}=0, so {\bm{\gamma}}_{t}={\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{stat}} or {\bm{1}}), those 128 rank units are assigned to the write gate (r_{w}=256) to hold R_{\mathrm{tot}}=512 and the layer parameter count invariant.

We also order the columns of {\bm{W}}_{\downarrow}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times 512} to match the 256-column MXU tile width and the 128-lane TPU VMEM tile boundary (Figure [3 c](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). Placing the two rank-64 gates (f,b) contiguously at columns [0\!:\!128] and the three rank-128 gates (g,w,q) contiguously at columns [128\!:\!512] executes the fused down-projection {\bm{h}}_{t}={\bm{x}}_{t}{\bm{W}}_{\downarrow} across two full 256\times 256 MXU tiles at 100\% column occupancy without zero-padding. At the same time, placing the split between the rank-64 and rank-128 groups at column 128 aligns with an exact 128-lane VMEM tile boundary, so the five up-projections {\bm{h}}^{m}_{t}{\bm{W}}_{m\uparrow} execute as two batched matrix multiplications (stacking the two 64\times d matrices for (f,b) and the three 128\times d matrices for (g,w,q)) instead of five separate narrow matrix multiplications.

##### Iso-Parameter FFN Reallocation.

The fused low-rank gate projection ([9](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.E9 "Equation 9 ‣ 4.1 Low-Rank Gating and Matched-Filter Readout ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) uses only \sum_{m}2dr_{m}=2dR_{\mathrm{tot}} parameters instead of 2d^{2}+512d in GDN-2. Under the 12d^{2} per-layer non-embedding parameter budget of the Transformer, setting d_{\mathrm{ffn}}(R_{\mathrm{tot}})=4d-R_{\mathrm{tot}} gives 4d^{2}+2dR_{\mathrm{tot}}+2d(4d-R_{\mathrm{tot}})=12d^{2} for every d and R_{\mathrm{tot}}, expanding the FFN width relative to GDN-2 (d_{\mathrm{ffn}}=3d-256) by \Delta d_{\mathrm{ffn}}=d-256 channels at R_{\mathrm{tot}}=512. At d=2{,}048 and R_{\mathrm{tot}}=512, replacing the gate projections in GDN-2 (9.44\text{M} parameters) with ([9](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.E9 "Equation 9 ‣ 4.1 Low-Rank Gating and Matched-Filter Readout ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) (2.10\text{M} parameters) reduces gate-projection parameters and FLOPs by 4.5\times (-77.8\%) and token-mixer projection parameters and FLOPs by 28.0\% (26.21\text{M}\to 18.87\text{M} parameters/layer). At fixed d_{\mathrm{ffn}}=5{,}888, this reduces full-layer non-embedding parameters and linear FLOPs by 14.6\% (50.33\text{M}\to 42.99\text{M} parameters/layer); under our primary iso-parameter budget, reinvesting the 7.34\text{M} saved parameters per layer widens d_{\mathrm{ffn}} from 5{,}888 to 7{,}680=30\times 256 (+1{,}792 channels, or +30.4\%; Figure [3 d](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")).

### 4.2 Boundary-Pivoted Chunkwise Kernel

Within each chunk of length C indexed by t\in\{1,\dotsc,C\}, starting from chunk-initial state {\bm{S}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{v}}, replacing {\bm{q}}_{t} with the gain-modulated query \hat{\bm{q}}_{t}={\bm{\gamma}}_{t}\odot{\bm{q}}_{t} reduces the Muir recurrence ([8](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.E8 "Equation 8 ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) to the chunkwise linear system of GDN-2 in §[2.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S2.SS2 "2.2 Chunkwise Parallel Form ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"). Let {\bm{\Lambda}}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{r=1}^{t}\log{\bm{\alpha}}_{r}\leq{\bm{0}} denote the cumulative log-decay at timestep t,3 3 3 In practice, instead of running a sequential prefix scan on the VPU, we multiply the chunk log-decay matrix \log{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}^{C\times d_{k}} (with rows \log{\bm{\alpha}}_{t}) by a lower-triangular all-ones matrix on the MXU: {\bm{\Lambda}}=\tril({\bm{1}}_{C\times C})\log{\bm{\alpha}}. with chunk-prefix and pairwise relative decays {\bm{\pi}}_{t}=\exp({\bm{\Lambda}}_{t}),\,{\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}=\exp({\bm{\Lambda}}_{t}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{s})\in(0,1]^{1\times d_{k}} (t\geq s). Evaluating a chunk requires three stages: (i) constructing the unit lower-triangular system matrix {\bm{T}}=\mathbf{I}+{\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}} with strictly lower-triangular entries ({\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}})_{t,s}=\langle\hat{\bm{k}}_{t},{\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}\odot{\bm{k}}_{s}\rangle (t>s) and the lower-triangular causal readout matrix {\bm{M}} with entries {M}_{t,s}=\langle\hat{\bm{q}}_{t},{\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}\odot{\bm{k}}_{s}\rangle (t\geq s); (ii) solving the triangular system {\bm{T}}{\bm{Z}}=\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{V}}&\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}\end{bmatrix} for {\bm{Z}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{U}}&{\bm{Y}}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{C\times(d_{v}+d_{k})}, where \hat{\bm{V}} and \hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi} have rows \hat{\bm{v}}_{t} and {\bm{\pi}}_{t}\odot\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}, respectively; and (iii) substituting \tilde{\bm{V}}={\bm{U}}-{\bm{Y}}{\bm{S}}_{0} into the chunkwise readout and state update to advance the chunk outputs {\bm{O}} and end-of-chunk state {\bm{S}}_{C} as affine maps of {\bm{S}}_{0}:

{\bm{O}}=\big(\hat{\bm{Q}}_{\odot\pi}-{\bm{M}}{\bm{Y}}\big){\bm{S}}_{0}+{\bm{M}}{\bm{U}},\qquad{\bm{S}}_{C}=\big(\Diag({\bm{\pi}}_{C})-\tilde{\bm{K}}^{\top}{\bm{Y}}\big){\bm{S}}_{0}+\tilde{\bm{K}}^{\top}{\bm{U}},(10)

where \hat{\bm{Q}}_{\odot\pi},\tilde{\bm{K}}\in\mathbb{R}^{C\times d_{k}} have rows (\hat{\bm{Q}}_{\odot\pi})_{t,:}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\bm{\pi}}_{t}\odot\hat{\bm{q}}_{t} and \tilde{\bm{K}}_{s,:}={\bm{\pi}}_{C,s}\odot{\bm{k}}_{s}.

##### Divide-and-Conquer Block Decomposition.

As noted in §[2.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S2.SS2 "2.2 Chunkwise Parallel Form ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), executing stages (i) and (ii) directly over the full chunk is bottlenecked by the C\times C\times d_{k} 3D decay tensor (or the underflowing division {\bm{\pi}}_{t}\oslash{\bm{\pi}}_{s}) when forming {\bm{T}} and {\bm{M}}, and by sequential C-step forward substitution when solving with {\bm{T}}. Instead of forward substitution, we construct {\bm{T}}^{-1} and {\bm{M}} via a divide-and-conquer recursion ([Heller, 1974](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib44); [Bini and Pan, 1994](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib15); [Sobczyk et al., 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib109)) over a binary tree of depth r and apply {\bm{T}}^{-1} as a matrix multiplication \begin{bmatrix}{\bm{U}}&{\bm{Y}}\end{bmatrix}={\bm{T}}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{V}}&\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}\end{bmatrix}. Whenever any contiguous token interval is split into left ({\mathcal{L}}) and right ({\mathcal{R}}) halves of equal length, the corresponding sub-blocks of {\bm{T}}, its inverse {\bm{T}}^{-1}, and {\bm{M}} partition into 2\times 2 block form:

{\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{blk}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}{\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{L}}&{\bm{0}}\\[2.0pt]
\hline\cr\\[-10.0pt]
{\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{cross}}&{\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{R}}\end{array}\right],\quad{\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{blk}}^{-1}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}{\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}&{\bm{0}}\\[2.0pt]
\hline\cr\\[-10.0pt]
-{\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}{\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{cross}}{\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}&{\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}\end{array}\right];\qquad{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{blk}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}{\bm{M}}_{\mathcal{L}}&{\bm{0}}\\[2.0pt]
\hline\cr\\[-10.0pt]
{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{cross}}&{\bm{M}}_{\mathcal{R}}\end{array}\right].(11)

Here, the diagonal sub-blocks ({\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{L}},{\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{R}}) and their inverses ({\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{L}}^{-1},{\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}) remain unit lower-triangular and ({\bm{M}}_{\mathcal{L}},{\bm{M}}_{\mathcal{R}}) remain lower-triangular, whereas the bottom-left cross-blocks {\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{cross}} and {\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{cross}} (whose row indices t\in{\mathcal{R}} and column indices s\in{\mathcal{L}} satisfy t>s for every entry) are dense. Because the diagonal sub-blocks preserve the exact same lower-triangular structure on half the interval, bisecting the chunk r times reduces stages (i) and (ii) to a base case on 2^{r} leaf intervals of length n_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}C/2^{r}, followed by r bottom-up merge levels (m=0,\dotsc,r-1) across child intervals of length n_{m}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}n_{0}2^{m}.

Base Case: Diagonal Leaf Blocks (n_{0}=C/2^{r}). Within each of the 2^{r} leaf intervals (\lceil t/n_{0}\rceil=\lceil s/n_{0}\rceil), we evaluate the pairwise decay {\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}=\exp({\bm{\Lambda}}_{t}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{s}) directly from the non-positive log-differences {\bm{\Lambda}}_{t}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{s}\leq{\bm{0}} (t\geq s) to form the diagonal leaf blocks {\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{leaf}}=\mathbf{I}+{\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{leaf}} and {\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{leaf}}. Because each leaf has size n_{0}\times n_{0}, forming the leaf decay tensors across all 2^{r} leaves materializes only 2^{r}(n_{0}^{2}d_{k})=Cn_{0}d_{k} elements per chunk, a 2^{r}\times reduction compared to the full C^{2}d_{k} 3D decay tensor. Moreover, because the strictly lower-triangular matrix {\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{leaf}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}\times n_{0}} is nilpotent of index n_{0} ({\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{leaf}}^{n_{0}}={\bm{0}}), its Neumann series terminates at degree n_{0}-1 and factors into \log_{2}n_{0} multiplicative stages (\log_{2}n_{0}-1 squaring steps; [Orcutt, 1974](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib79); [Heller, 1978](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib45)):

{\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{leaf}}^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{n_{0}-1}(-{\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{leaf}})^{k}=\prod_{j=0}^{\log_{2}n_{0}-1}\big(\mathbf{I}+(-{\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{leaf}})^{2^{j}}\big),(12)

where n_{0}\ll C bounds nilpotent matrix powers in bfloat16([Sobczyk et al., 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib109)).

Inductive Step: Bottom-Up Merge via Boundary-Pivoted Splitting (n_{m}=n_{0}2^{m}). At each merge level (m=0,\dotsc,r-1), a parent interval of length 2n_{m} merges adjacent left ({\mathcal{L}}) and right ({\mathcal{R}}) child intervals of length n_{m}, for which the diagonal blocks ({\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{L}}^{-1},{\bm{M}}_{\mathcal{L}}) and ({\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1},{\bm{M}}_{\mathcal{R}}) have already been computed. By ([11](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ Divide-and-Conquer Block Decomposition. ‣ 4.2 Boundary-Pivoted Chunkwise Kernel ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), completing the parent blocks requires forming the dense n_{m}\times n_{m} cross-blocks {\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{cross}} and {\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{cross}} and multiplying -{\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}{\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{cross}}{\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{L}}^{-1} for the bottom-left block of {\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{blk}}^{-1}. To compute {\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{cross}} and {\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{cross}} via matrix multiplications without dividing by {\bm{\pi}}_{s}=\exp({\bm{\Lambda}}_{s}), we exploit the fact that every cross-interval pair (t,s)\in{\mathcal{R}}\times{\mathcal{L}} straddles the interval boundary between {\mathcal{L}} and {\mathcal{R}}. Extending sub-chunk boundary decay splitting ([Yang et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib124); [Yang and Zhang, 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib123); [Kimi Team et al., 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib61)) to every merge level of the tree, we choose the _boundary pivot_ p\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{last}({\mathcal{L}}) with cumulative log-decay {\bm{\Lambda}}_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times{d_{k}}} and split the log-decay difference across p:

{\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}=\exp({\bm{\Lambda}}_{t}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{s})=\exp\big(({\bm{\Lambda}}_{t}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{p})+({\bm{\Lambda}}_{p}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{s})\big)=\underbrace{\exp({\bm{\Lambda}}_{t}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{p})}_{{\bm{\pi}}_{t,p}}\odot\underbrace{\exp({\bm{\Lambda}}_{p}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{s})}_{{\bm{\pi}}_{p,s}},\qquad t\in{\mathcal{R}},\;s\in{\mathcal{L}}.

Because cumulative log-decay is monotonically non-increasing ({\bm{\Lambda}}_{1}\geq\dotsb\geq{\bm{\Lambda}}_{C}) and s\leq p<t for all s\in{\mathcal{L}} and t\in{\mathcal{R}}, both {\bm{\Lambda}}_{t}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{p}\leq{\bm{0}} and {\bm{\Lambda}}_{p}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{s}\leq{\bm{0}} hold identically, keeping both exponential factors in (0,1] without division or overflow. Each cross-block entry therefore decouples across p as ({\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}})_{t,s}=(\hat{\bm{k}}_{t}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{t,p})({\bm{k}}_{s}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{p,s})^{\top} and {M}_{t,s}=(\hat{\bm{q}}_{t}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{t,p})({\bm{k}}_{s}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{p,s})^{\top}. Stacking {\bm{\pi}}_{t,p} (t\in{\mathcal{R}}) and {\bm{\pi}}_{p,s} (s\in{\mathcal{L}}) as the rows of {\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{R}},{\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{L}}\in(0,1]^{n_{m}\times d_{k}} computes both {\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{cross}} and {\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{cross}} in a single row-packed matrix multiplication sharing ({\bm{K}}_{\mathcal{L}}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{L}})^{\top}, followed by two matrix multiplications for the bottom-left inverse block {\bm{X}}_{21}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}({\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{blk}}^{-1})_{{\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{L}}} in ([11](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ Divide-and-Conquer Block Decomposition. ‣ 4.2 Boundary-Pivoted Chunkwise Kernel ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")):

\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{cross}}\\
{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{cross}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{K}}_{\mathcal{R}}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{R}}\\
\hat{\bm{Q}}_{\mathcal{R}}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{R}}\end{bmatrix}\big({\bm{K}}_{\mathcal{L}}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{L}}\big)^{\top},\qquad{\bm{X}}_{21}=\big({\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{blk}}^{-1}\big)_{{\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{L}}}=-{\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}{\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{cross}}{\bm{T}}_{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}.

After r merge levels, {\bm{T}}^{-1} and {\bm{M}} are assembled across the full chunk without materializing {\bm{T}} via prefix division, yielding \begin{bmatrix}{\bm{U}}&{\bm{Y}}\end{bmatrix}={\bm{T}}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{V}}&\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}\end{bmatrix} and the affine state update ([10](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.E10 "Equation 10 ‣ 4.2 Boundary-Pivoted Chunkwise Kernel ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")).

##### Custom Backward Pass.

In the backward pass, differentiating the chunk readout and state update ([10](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.E10 "Equation 10 ‣ 4.2 Boundary-Pivoted Chunkwise Kernel ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) in stage (iii) requires only matrix multiplications to compute the gradients \mathrm{d}{\bm{Z}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{d}{\bm{U}}&\mathrm{d}{\bm{Y}}\end{bmatrix} and \mathrm{d}{\bm{M}} (where \mathrm{d}{\bm{X}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\partial{\mathcal{J}}/\partial{\bm{X}} for scalar loss {\mathcal{J}}). However, naively differentiating through stages (i) and (ii) via automatic differentiation would retain all r tree levels of intermediate inverse sub-blocks and scaled key/query tensors in HBM and backpropagate sequentially through the r block-inversion levels ([11](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ Divide-and-Conquer Block Decomposition. ‣ 4.2 Boundary-Pivoted Chunkwise Kernel ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) and \log_{2}n_{0}-1 leaf-squaring steps. Instead, we define a custom backward pass for stages (i) and (ii) that saves only the assembled inverse {\bm{T}}^{-1} and solved right-hand side {\bm{Z}} along with the chunk inputs.

First, to backpropagate through stage (ii), differentiating {\bm{T}}{\bm{Z}}=\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{V}}&\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}\end{bmatrix} with {\bm{T}}=\mathbf{I}+{\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}} yields the gradients \mathrm{d}\!\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{V}}&\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}\end{bmatrix} and \mathrm{d}{\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}}\in\mathbb{R}^{C\times C} directly from the saved {\bm{T}}^{-1} and {\bm{Z}} in two full-chunk MXU matrix multiplications without backpropagating through the forward inversion tree:

\mathrm{d}\!\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{V}}&\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}\end{bmatrix}={\bm{T}}^{-\top}\mathrm{d}{\bm{Z}},\qquad\mathrm{d}{\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}}=-\stril\!\left(\mathrm{d}\!\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{V}}&\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}\end{bmatrix}{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\right),

where \mathrm{d}\!\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{V}}&\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}\end{bmatrix}{\bm{Z}}^{\top} uses three-pass bfloat16 MXU multiplication with float32 accumulation so that the log-decay gradients driven by \mathrm{d}{\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}} retain high accuracy without falling back to the VPU.

Second, to backpropagate (\mathrm{d}{\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}},\mathrm{d}{\bm{M}}) through stage (i) to (\mathrm{d}\hat{\bm{Q}},\mathrm{d}{\bm{K}},\mathrm{d}\hat{\bm{K}},\mathrm{d}{\bm{\Lambda}}) without storing intermediate tree activations in HBM, we traverse the same depth-r boundary-pivoted binary tree and differentiate the 2^{r} diagonal leaves of size n_{0} in parallel across leaves. At each merge level m\in\{0,\dotsc,r-1\} with child size n_{m}=n_{0}2^{m}, we recompute the boundary-pivot scalings {\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{R}}=\exp({\bm{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{R}}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{p}) and {\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{L}}=\exp({\bm{\Lambda}}_{p}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{L}}) on the fly in VMEM from {\bm{\Lambda}} and compute the scaled query and key gradients from the off-diagonal blocks \mathrm{d}{\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{cross}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}})_{{\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{L}}} and \mathrm{d}{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{cross}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\mathrm{d}{\bm{M}})_{{\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{L}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{m}\times n_{m}} in two row-packed MXU matrix multiplications:

\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{d}(\hat{\bm{K}}_{\mathcal{R}}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{R}})\\
\mathrm{d}(\hat{\bm{Q}}_{\mathcal{R}}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{R}})\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{d}{\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{cross}}\\
\mathrm{d}{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{cross}}\end{bmatrix}\big({\bm{K}}_{\mathcal{L}}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{L}}\big),\qquad\mathrm{d}({\bm{K}}_{\mathcal{L}}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{L}})=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{d}{\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{cross}}\\
\mathrm{d}{\bm{M}}_{\mathrm{cross}}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{K}}_{\mathcal{R}}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{R}}\\
\hat{\bm{Q}}_{\mathcal{R}}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{R}}\end{bmatrix},

before applying the elementwise chain rule through {\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{R}} and {\bm{\pi}}_{\mathcal{L}} into (\mathrm{d}\hat{\bm{Q}},\mathrm{d}{\bm{K}},\mathrm{d}\hat{\bm{K}},\mathrm{d}{\bm{\Lambda}}) and converting \mathrm{d}{\bm{\Lambda}} to \mathrm{d}\log{\bm{\alpha}}=\tril({\bm{1}}_{C\times C})^{\top}\mathrm{d}{\bm{\Lambda}}=\triu({\bm{1}}_{C\times C})\mathrm{d}{\bm{\Lambda}} on the MXU (full stage-(iii) backward scan equations and elementwise exponential derivatives are in [Section F.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A6.SS2 "F.2 Chunkwise Backward Pass ‣ Appendix F Chunkwise Kernel Analysis ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")).

![Image 4: Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376v1/figs/fig4_kernel_design.png)

Figure 4: Boundary-pivoted chunkwise kernel eliminates 3D decay tensors, division overflow, and VPU bottlenecks. (a) Memory hierarchy of a TPU7x chip. (b) On the 256\times 256 MXU, an unfused 256\times 128 right-hand side \hat{\bm{V}} achieves only 50\% utilization due to zero-padded lanes, whereas the fused solve \begin{bmatrix}{\bm{U}}&{\bm{Y}}\end{bmatrix}={\bm{T}}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{V}}&\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}\end{bmatrix} achieves 100\% utilization. (c) At chunk size C=256, leaf size 8, and d_{k}=d_{v}=128, replacing naive C\times C\times d_{k} 3D VPU decay and sequential VPU triangular solves with boundary-pivoted MXU matrix multiplications, recursive block inversion, and a custom backward pass cuts forward-and-backward VPU compute by 24\times and total kernel FLOPs by 12\%, routing 98\% of chunkwise arithmetic onto the MXU. (d) Boundary-pivoted chunkwise execution combines pre-permutation VREG fusion for \hat{\bm{q}}_{t} and \hat{\bm{v}}_{t}, MXU cumulative log-decay, numerically stable VPU decay splitting, recursive block inversion of {\bm{T}} on the MXU, and a packed MXU scan carrying {\bm{S}}_{0}\to{\bm{S}}_{C} in VMEM (saving {\bm{S}}_{0} only for backward).

##### TPU Implementation and Batched Execution.

Figure [4 d](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Custom Backward Pass. ‣ 4.2 Boundary-Pivoted Chunkwise Kernel ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") traces how this algorithm maps onto the TPU7x memory hierarchy (Figure [4 a](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Custom Backward Pass. ‣ 4.2 Boundary-Pivoted Chunkwise Kernel ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). The projections and depthwise convolutions produce activations in token-major order (B,T,N_{h},d_{k}), whereas chunkwise execution operates on chunks of length C per head and requires permuting axes into chunk-major order (N_{c},B,N_{h},C,d_{k}) with T=N_{c}C. Because permuting the trailing tiled dimensions from (N_{h},d_{k}) to (C,d_{k}) incurs an HBM round-trip per tensor, and ([8](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.E8 "Equation 8 ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) depends on ({\bm{q}}_{t},{\bm{\gamma}}_{t}) and ({\bm{v}}_{t},{\bm{w}}_{t}) only through \hat{\bm{q}}_{t}={\bm{\gamma}}_{t}\odot{\bm{q}}_{t} and \hat{\bm{v}}_{t}={\bm{w}}_{t}\odot{\bm{v}}_{t}, we fuse \hat{\bm{q}}_{t} and \hat{\bm{v}}_{t} in VREGs before permutation (Figure [3 b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) alongside \hat{\bm{k}}_{t}={\bm{b}}_{t}\odot{\bm{k}}_{t} (retaining {\bm{k}}_{t}), reducing the token-to-chunk permutation from seven tensors to five: (\log{\bm{\alpha}},\hat{\bm{V}},{\bm{K}},\hat{\bm{K}},\hat{\bm{Q}}).

We set C=256 to match the 256\times 256 MXU tile size and n_{0}=8 (r=5 levels, 32 leaves per chunk) to match the 8-row 32-bit T(8, 128) VMEM tile for float32 leaf buffers during bfloat16 MXU squaring (before casting {\bm{T}}_{\mathrm{leaf}}^{-1} to bfloat16); Figure [4 d](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Custom Backward Pass. ‣ 4.2 Boundary-Pivoted Chunkwise Kernel ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") illustrates a two-level tree with four leaves {\bm{T}}_{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}}}^{-1},{\bm{T}}_{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}}}^{-1},{\bm{T}}_{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}}}^{-1},{\bm{T}}_{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}}^{-1} and off-diagonal inverse blocks {\bm{X}}_{{\mathcal{L}},21},{\bm{X}}_{{\mathcal{R}},21}, and {\bm{X}}_{21}. To evaluate all nodes at a given tree depth simultaneously, reshaping the chunk axis into (32,8) at the leaf level folds all 32 leaves into the leading batch dimensions (N_{c},B,N_{h},32), so leaf evaluation and the two Neumann squaring steps run in parallel across all leaves, chunks, and heads. Likewise, at each merge level m\in\{0,\dotsc,4\}, reshaping the chunk axis into (N_{m},2n_{m}) with N_{m}=2^{4-m} parent nodes folds all N_{m} merges across all chunks and heads into batched MXU contractions, so the full tree completes in r=5 sequential stages in both the forward and backward passes.

Once {\bm{T}}^{-1} and {\bm{M}} are assembled, concatenating \begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{V}}&\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{256\times 256} along columns (for d_{k}=d_{v}=128) computes \begin{bmatrix}{\bm{U}}&{\bm{Y}}\end{bmatrix}={\bm{T}}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{V}}&\hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}\end{bmatrix} (and {\bm{T}}^{-\top}\mathrm{d}{\bm{Z}} in the backward pass) in a single 256\times 256 MXU matrix multiplication per chunk at 100\% tile utilization, whereas unfused 256\times 128 solves drop to 50\% utilization (Figure [4 b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Custom Backward Pass. ‣ 4.2 Boundary-Pivoted Chunkwise Kernel ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). Similarly, stacking {\bm{M}} and \tilde{\bm{K}}^{\top} along rows into a (C+d_{k})\times C matrix multiplies \begin{bmatrix}{\bm{U}}&{\bm{Y}}\end{bmatrix} in one packed MXU contraction across all chunks in parallel to form ({\bm{P}}_{\mathrm{read}},{\bm{P}}_{\mathrm{bias}}) of ([10](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.E10 "Equation 10 ‣ 4.2 Boundary-Pivoted Chunkwise Kernel ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")):

\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{O}}\\
{\bm{S}}_{C}\end{bmatrix}=\underbrace{\left(\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{Q}}_{\odot\pi}\\
\Diag({\bm{\pi}}_{C})\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{M}}\\
\tilde{\bm{K}}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}{\bm{Y}}\right)}_{{\bm{P}}_{\mathrm{read}}}{\bm{S}}_{0}+\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{M}}\\
\tilde{\bm{K}}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}{\bm{U}}}_{{\bm{P}}_{\mathrm{bias}}}.

Because the per-head state {\bm{S}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{128\times 128} (float32, 64\text{ KB} per head) fits in on-chip VMEM (128\text{ MB} per chip), this packed scan carries {\bm{S}}_{0}\to{\bm{S}}_{C} in VMEM during the forward sweep and writes chunk-boundary {\bm{S}}_{0} to HBM only for the backward pass. Over a full training step (Figure [4 c](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Custom Backward Pass. ‣ 4.2 Boundary-Pivoted Chunkwise Kernel ‣ 4 Muir ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and [Section F.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A6.SS1 "F.1 MXU and VPU FLOP Breakdown ‣ Appendix F Chunkwise Kernel Analysis ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), this kernel executes 1.37\text{M} MXU FLOPs (98\%) and only 29.7\text{K} VPU FLOPs (2\%) per token per head, which reduces VPU compute by 24\times and total chunkwise FLOPs by 12\% relative to naive chunkwise execution.

## 5 Experiments

We detail our experimental setup in §[5.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.SS1 "5.1 Experimental Setup ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), present main results on language modeling, recall, length extrapolation, training throughput, and numerical stability in §[5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.SS2 "5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), and ablate Muir in §[5.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.SS3 "5.3 Ablations ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout").

### 5.1 Experimental Setup

##### Models and Baselines.

We evaluate all models across four iso-parameter scales: 166\text{M} (L=8,d=1{,}024), 406\text{M} (L=11,d=1{,}536), 834\text{M} (L=14,d=2{,}048), and 1.34\text{B} (L=24,d=2{,}048), where all architectures at a given scale share depth L, residual width d, head dimension d_{k}=d_{v}=128 (N_{h}=d/128 heads), a \mathrm{GELU} FFN, and untied input and output embeddings (full per-scale configurations are provided in [Section G.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A7.SS1 "G.1 Pretraining Setup ‣ Appendix G Training Setup and Model Configurations ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). Under the 12d^{2} per-layer non-embedding parameter budget, we compare five model variants across all four scales: (i) Transformer, with rotary position embeddings (\mathrm{RoPE},\theta=10{,}000; [Su et al., 2021](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib110)), FFN width d_{\mathrm{ffn}}=4d, and TPU Pallas Splash Attention ([Dao et al., 2022](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib28));4 4 4[https://github.com/jax-ml/jax/tree/main/jax/experimental/pallas/ops/tpu/splash_attention](https://github.com/jax-ml/jax/tree/main/jax/experimental/pallas/ops/tpu/splash_attention). (ii) GDN-2, the channel-wise gated delta recurrence with rank-128(f,g) gates, dense d\times d(b,w) gates, and d_{\mathrm{ffn}}=3d-256;5 5 5 Under the same 1.34\text{B} FineWeb-Edu setup, [Hatamizadeh et al. (2026)](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib42) showed that GDN-2 consistently outperforms scalar-decay Gated DeltaNet ([Yang et al., 2025b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib126)), Kimi Linear ([Kimi Team et al., 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib61)), and Mamba-3 ([Lahoti et al., 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib63)) across language modeling and retrieval, so we use GDN-2 as our recurrent baseline. (iii) Muir (no {\bm{\gamma}}), which replaces the gate projections in GDN-2 with the fused low-rank projection of ranks (r_{f},r_{b},r_{g},r_{w},r_{q})=(64,64,128,256,0) and unit readout ({\bm{\gamma}}_{t}={\bm{1}}); (iv) Muir (static {\bm{\gamma}}), which adds the learned static readout gain {\bm{\gamma}}_{t}={\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{stat}} at the same gate ranks; and (v) Muir (ours), which splits the 256-column write budget into (r_{w},r_{q})=(128,128) to parameterize the dynamic readout gain {\bm{\gamma}}_{t}. Across all recurrent models (ii)–(v), the chunkwise recurrence is executed with chunk length C=256, and all three Muir variants (iii)–(v) share total gate rank R_{\mathrm{tot}}=512 and FFN width d_{\mathrm{ffn}}=4d-512.

##### Pretraining Recipe.

Following [Hatamizadeh et al. (2026)](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib42), we pretrain all models from scratch on 100\text{B} tokens sampled from FineWeb-Edu ([Penedo et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib82)), tokenized with the 32{,}000-token Llama-2 SentencePiece tokenizer ([Touvron et al., 2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib114)) and presented in identical token order across all runs. We optimize all models using AdamW ([Loshchilov and Hutter, 2017](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib69)) (\beta_{1}=0.9,\beta_{2}=0.999) with weight decay 0.1 on weight matrices (0.0 on additive biases, a_{\log}, and {\bm{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{stat}}), global gradient norm clipping at 1.0, and a 1\text{B}-token (1\%) linear warmup to a peak learning rate of 4\times 10^{-4} followed by cosine decay to 4\times 10^{-5} (0.1\times peak). Training runs on 8 TPU7x chips in bfloat16 mixed precision (with float32 master weights, recurrent state {\bm{S}}_{t}, and cumulative log-decay {\bm{\Lambda}}_{t}) at sequence length T=4{,}096 and a global batch size of 524{,}288\text{ tokens/step}.

##### Evaluation.

We evaluate language modeling and commonsense reasoning using the lm-evaluation-harness([Gao et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib35)). We report held-out FineWeb-Edu validation _token_ negative log-likelihood (NLL, over 100\text{M} tokens) and _word-level_ perplexity on WikiText ([Merity et al., 2016](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib73)) and LAMBADA ([Paperno et al., 2016](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib81)). We also report zero-shot accuracy on LAMBADA, PIQA ([Bisk et al., 2020](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib18)), HellaSwag ([Zellers et al., 2019](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib128)), WinoGrande ([Sakaguchi et al., 2021](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib98)), ARC-Easy and ARC-Challenge ([Clark et al., 2018](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib25)), OpenBookQA ([Mihaylov et al., 2018](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib74)), and Social IQA ([Sap et al., 2019](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib100)), as well as 5-shot WinoGrande and closed-book TriviaQA ([Joshi et al., 2017](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib57)). Following [Yang et al. (2025b)](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib126); [Lahoti et al. (2026)](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib63), we report length-normalized accuracy for HellaSwag and ARC-Challenge, and unnormalized accuracy for all other tasks.6 6 6 We omit zero-shot BoolQ ([Clark et al., 2019](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib24)) from the main text because it hovers near its 62.2\% majority-class baseline with high single-checkpoint variance across sub-1.5\text{B} models ([Madaan et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib71); [Miller, 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib75); [Heineman et al., 2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib43)); BoolQ accuracy and the 9-task average are reported in [Section H.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A8.SS1 "H.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning ‣ Appendix H Evaluation Protocols and Supplementary Results ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout").

To evaluate associative recall, we test synthetic retrieval and state tracking on six tasks from RULER ([Hsieh et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib49)) (500 examples per task and context length, spanning uniform needle depths 0\text{--}100\%): single-needle retrieval (S-NIAH-1/2/3, testing 7-digit numbers and 36-character UUIDs in synthetic noise and Paul Graham essays), as well as 5-hop variable tracking (VT), at context lengths 1\text{K}\text{--}8\text{K} (2\times the 4\text{K} training window), alongside multi-value (MV-NIAH, 4 values bound to one key) and multi-query (MQ-NIAH, 4 distinct query keys) retrieval across the in-distribution lengths 1\text{K}\text{--}4\text{K} (additional details in [Section H.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A8.SS2 "H.2 Associative Recall ‣ Appendix H Evaluation Protocols and Supplementary Results ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). We also evaluate real-world extractive recall at 2\text{K} context on the six Based/JRT benchmarks ([Arora et al., 2024a](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib5); [Arora et al., 2024b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib6)): SWDE ([Lockard et al., 2019](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib68)), SQuAD ([Rajpurkar et al., 2018](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib94)), FDA ([Arora et al., 2023b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib4)), TriviaQA ([Joshi et al., 2017](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib57)), Natural Questions ([Kwiatkowski et al., 2019](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib62)), and DROP ([Dua et al., 2019](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib32)).

For length extrapolation, we test the 1.34\text{B} models both zero-shot from 4\text{K} pretraining and after a 300-step 64\text{K} cooldown (0.315\text{B} held-out FineWeb-Edu tokens, or 0.3\% of pretraining, using a 70/30 token mix of \geq\!16\text{K} and standard documents; [Fu et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib34)). Our Transformer baseline uses training-free Dynamic YaRN ([Peng et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib85)) beyond 4\text{K} in the zero-shot regime and raises the RoPE base to \theta=5\times 10^{5} during cooldown ([Xiong et al., 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib121)) with Dynamic YaRN beyond 64\text{K} ([Section G.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A7.SS2 "G.2 Long-Context Cooldown and Position Extrapolation ‣ Appendix G Training Setup and Model Configurations ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). Language-modeling extrapolation covers 64 contiguous 128\text{K}-token documents (S=128\text{K},T\in\{4\text{K},\dotsc,128\text{K}\}) and three continuous 10\text{M}-token streams (S=10\text{M},T\in\{4\text{K},\dotsc,10\text{M}\}) from FineWeb-Edu validation, where each sequence is partitioned into S/T non-overlapping reset windows so every T scores identical target tokens; to separate long-range state drift from fewer boundary resets, we pair 128\text{K} perplexity with \Delta_{\mathrm{tail}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{NLL}_{[127\text{K},128\text{K})}(T=128\text{K})-\mathrm{NLL}_{[127\text{K},128\text{K})}(T=4\text{K}). Long-context passkey retrieval is evaluated on RULER S-NIAH-1 from 1\text{K} to 1\text{M} tokens (160 examples across 10 uniform depth bins at each of 11 context lengths).

Finally, we measure end-to-end training throughput (K tokens/s) on 8 TPU7x chips at a fixed batch size of 524{,}288\text{ tokens/step} across sequence lengths T\in\{2\text{K},4\text{K},8\text{K},16\text{K},32\text{K}\} for Muir against GDN-2 and Transformer. We also verify the numerical accuracy and conditioning of the boundary-pivoted C=256 chunkwise kernel against an exact float64 sequential reference.

### 5.2 Results

#### 5.2.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning

Across all four model scales ([Figures 5](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F5 "In Pretraining and Scaling Dynamics. ‣ 5.2.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.T1 "Table 1 ‣ Pretraining and Scaling Dynamics. ‣ 5.2.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), all three Muir variants outperform GDN-2 and Transformer on validation NLL, WikiText perplexity, and the 8-task zero-shot average.

##### Pretraining and Scaling Dynamics.

In [Figure 5](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F5 "In Pretraining and Scaling Dynamics. ‣ 5.2.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), all three Muir variants improve over GDN-2 within the first 10\text{B} tokens and reach a 12\text{--}20\text{ millinat (mnat)} reduction by 100\text{B} tokens because reallocating the freed gate parameters to widen the FFN (from d_{\mathrm{ffn}}=3d-256 to 4d-512) expands token-level representation capacity from the start of optimization. As training proceeds and the recurrent heads specialize their decay gates {\bm{\alpha}}_{t} across timescales, the resulting stationary row-energy imbalance ([Proposition 3.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition 3.4 (Stationary row-energy law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) creates headroom for the matched-filter readout ([Propositions 3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[3.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem8 "Corollary 3.8 (Horizon-marginalized static gain and fractional power law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), and static and dynamic {\bm{\gamma}}_{t} maintain consistent additional gains over Muir (no {\bm{\gamma}}) throughout training. Across all four scales, Muir reaches the 100\text{B}-token validation NLL of GDN-2 by roughly 85\text{B} tokens (1.2\times faster), and at 1.34\text{B} ([Table 1](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.T1 "In Pretraining and Scaling Dynamics. ‣ 5.2.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), full Muir lowers final validation NLL by 20.4\text{ mnats} relative to GDN-2 and by 36.2\text{ mnats} relative to Transformer, with matched-filter readout accounting for 3.3\text{ mnats} of the improvement over Muir (no {\bm{\gamma}}).

![Image 5: Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376v1/figs/fig5_nll_curves.png)

Figure 5: Muir consistently lowers validation NLL relative to GDN-2 and Transformer throughout \bm{100\text{B}}-token pretraining. Held-out FineWeb-Edu validation token NLL difference relative to GDN-2 across 166\text{M}\text{--}1.34\text{B} parameters as a function of training tokens. All three Muir variants improve over GDN-2 within the first 10\text{B} tokens and reach a 12\text{--}20\text{ mnat} reduction by 100\text{B} tokens, while static and dynamic matched-filter readout provide additional reductions over Muir (no {\bm{\gamma}}).

Table 1: Muir improves language modeling and downstream reasoning over Transformer and GDN-2 across model scale. We report FineWeb-Edu validation token NLL (FW-Edu), WikiText (Wiki.) and LAMBADA (LMB.) word-level perplexity, zero-shot accuracy across eight commonsense reasoning benchmarks and their mean (Avg.), and 5-shot WinoGrande (Wino.) and TriviaQA (TQA) accuracy after 100\text{B} training tokens. Best results per scale are in bold and second-best are underlined.

##### Short-Context Parametric Knowledge and Context-Dependent Readout.

Standard zero-shot multiple-choice benchmarks (PIQA, HellaSwag, WinoGrande, ARC, OBQA, and SIQA) use short prompts (<\!100 tokens) that place little load on long-range state retention and mainly probe world knowledge in FFN weights ([Geva et al., 2021](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib36); [Meng et al., 2022](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib72)). Whereas GDN-2 improves temporal mixing over Transformer (d_{\mathrm{ffn}}=4d) at the cost of a 28\text{--}31\% narrower FFN, Muir retains the gated-delta mixer while restoring near-Transformer FFN width; with this shared FFN expansion, all three Muir variants achieve comparable gains over GDN-2 and Transformer across scales. At 1.34\text{B}, Muir outperforms GDN-2 on all eight zero-shot tasks and raises the 8-task average by +1.38 points over GDN-2 (+1.79 over Transformer), while the three Muir variants remain within single-checkpoint sampling noise (0.4\text{--}0.7 points) at smaller scales.

By contrast, tasks that require resolving predictions from extended context isolate the readout filter. On LAMBADA word-level perplexity (predicting the final word of a passage from the preceding context) and on 5-shot WinoGrande and closed-book TriviaQA (extracting task formatting and entity-answer patterns across five in-context demonstrations while suppressing interference from earlier examples), FFN expansion alone (Muir no {\bm{\gamma}}) provides almost no gain over GDN-2 at 1.34\text{B} (10.26 vs. 10.38 on LAMBADA perplexity; 16.52\% vs. 16.48\% on 5-shot TriviaQA). Introducing the dynamic readout gain {\bm{\gamma}}_{t} drives the improvement on these context-sensitive evaluations, lowering 1.34\text{B} LAMBADA perplexity to 10.00 (achieving the lowest perplexity at 166\text{M},834\text{M}, and 1.34\text{B})7 7 7 Because LAMBADA scores only the final word of 5{,}153 passages, its perplexity has higher variance at smaller scales (\pm 0.67 SE at 406\text{M}, where Muir no {\bm{\gamma}} attains 18.11 vs. 20.00 for Muir despite the lower validation NLL of Muir). and raising 5-shot TriviaQA accuracy to 19.32\%. Consistent with [Propositions 3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[3.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem7 "Remark 3.7 (Incompatibility of static gain across retrieval lags). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), processing a multi-shot prompt requires querying both local syntactic context at short lags and demonstration patterns at lags of several hundred tokens, a bimodal retrieval mixture that dynamic {\bm{\gamma}}_{t} resolves where a static gain vector cannot.

![Image 6: Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376v1/figs/fig6_recall.png)

Figure 6: Muir improves synthetic single-needle, multi-hop, multi-needle, and real-world retrieval over GDN-2. (a) Averaged across the three single-needle tasks, all Muir variants mitigate the 4\text{K} degradation of GDN-2 and extrapolate to 8\text{K}, where unextended RoPE Transformer degrades beyond its 4\text{K} training window. (b) On 5-hop variable-binding chains, static {\bm{\gamma}} fails to match the varying hop lags, whereas dynamic {\bm{\gamma}}_{t} leads all models at every context length. (c, d) When retrieving four values bound to a single key or four distinct keys queried jointly, dynamic {\bm{\gamma}}_{t} matches or outperforms both GDN-2 and Transformer across 1\text{K}\text{--}4\text{K}. (e) Averaged across the RULER tasks (a–d) by needle depth and context length, Muir achieves the largest gains over GDN-2 on early needles (0\text{--}40\% depth) at 4\text{K}\text{--}8\text{K}. (f) On real-world document extraction at 2\text{K}, Muir improves over GDN-2 on all six tasks, with dynamic {\bm{\gamma}}_{t} driving 69\% of the gain.

#### 5.2.2 Associative Recall

[Figure 6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F6 "In Short-Context Parametric Knowledge and Context-Dependent Readout. ‣ 5.2.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") evaluates synthetic retrieval across the six RULER tasks and real-world document extraction across the six Based/JRT tasks at 1.34\text{B} parameters (complete per-task and per-length tables are in [Section H.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A8.SS2 "H.2 Associative Recall ‣ Appendix H Evaluation Protocols and Supplementary Results ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")).

##### Single-Needle, Multi-Hop, and Multi-Needle Retrieval.

Across the three single-needle tasks (S-NIAH-1/2/3; Figure [6 a](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ Short-Context Parametric Knowledge and Context-Dependent Readout. ‣ 5.2.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), unweighted readout in GDN-2 degrades as context length increases.8 8 8 While our GDN-2 baseline matches [Hatamizadeh et al. (2026)](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib42) on language modeling and zero-shot tasks, S-NIAH scores differ because we use lm-evaluation-harness instead of NVIDIA/RULER ([Section H.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A8.SS2 "H.2 Associative Recall ‣ Appendix H Evaluation Protocols and Supplementary Results ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). As established in [Propositions 3.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem10 "Proposition 3.10 (Erase-readout substitution). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[3.11](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem11 "Remark 3.11 (Mechanism of memory-horizon extension). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), unit readout ({\bm{\gamma}}_{t}={\bm{1}}) forces the recurrence to rely on heavier write-time erasure \bar{b} to keep multi-write interference bounded in high-energy rows, which shortens the effective memory horizon \tau_{\mathrm{eff},j}(\bar{b}) and flushes distant needles over thousands of intervening tokens. Calibrating channel readout ([Propositions 3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 3.6 (Lag-dependent matched-filter readout). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[3.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem8 "Corollary 3.8 (Horizon-marginalized static gain and fractional power law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) lowers the equilibrium erase rate and raises the 1\text{K}\text{--}8\text{K} single-needle mean over both baselines, with Muir holding 100\% on S-NIAH-1 through 8\text{K}. Because a single needle is queried at one depth at the end of the prompt, a fixed channel filter (Muir static {\bm{\gamma}}) suffices to suppress fast-row noise on single-key lookups, and pairing static {\bm{\gamma}} with the wider write gate (r_{w}=256 vs. 128 in full Muir at fixed R_{\mathrm{tot}}=512) reaches a slightly higher S-NIAH-1/2/3 average, particularly on S-NIAH-3 where writing a 36-character UUID across roughly 34 tokens benefits from higher write-gate rank.

By contrast, resolving a 5-hop pointer chain on VT (Figure [6 b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ Short-Context Parametric Knowledge and Context-Dependent Readout. ‣ 5.2.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) or sequentially retrieving four values on MV-NIAH (Figure [6 c](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ Short-Context Parametric Knowledge and Context-Dependent Readout. ‣ 5.2.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) and MQ-NIAH (Figure [6 d](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ Short-Context Parametric Knowledge and Context-Dependent Readout. ‣ 5.2.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) requires querying associations stored at multiple depths and shifting the readout filter across state channels as each answer token is generated. Because a single static gain vector cannot match multiple retrieval lags simultaneously ([Remark 3.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem7 "Remark 3.7 (Incompatibility of static gain across retrieval lags). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), static {\bm{\gamma}} falls below unweighted Muir on VT and below GDN-2 on MV-NIAH. Reallocating half of the write-gate rank to parameterize token-dependent {\bm{\gamma}}_{t} trades single-UUID write capacity for lag-adaptive readout, enabling Muir to outperform all recurrent and attention baselines and achieve the highest overall RULER suite average.

##### Depth Horizon and Real-World Extractive Recall.

In the depth-by-length breakdown (Figure [6 e](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ Short-Context Parametric Knowledge and Context-Dependent Readout. ‣ 5.2.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), both GDN-2 and Muir maintain uniform recall across needle depths at 1\text{K}\text{--}2\text{K} (with Muir leading by 7\text{--}12 points), whereas decoupling query-time noise suppression from write-time erasure ([Proposition 3.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem10 "Proposition 3.10 (Erase-readout substitution). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")) prevents the premature overwriting that causes GDN-2 accuracy to drop sharply on early-context needles (0\text{--}40\% depth) at 4\text{K}\text{--}8\text{K}. Similarly, on Based/JRT (Figure [6 f](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ Short-Context Parametric Knowledge and Context-Dependent Readout. ‣ 5.2.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), extracting semi-structured attributes (SWDE, FDA) and passage spans (SQuAD, TriviaQA, NQ, DROP) from natural documents requires resolving query-target lags that vary widely across examples. Consequently, FFN expansion (Muir no {\bm{\gamma}}) and static scaling (Muir static {\bm{\gamma}}) yield only modest gains over GDN-2, whereas dynamic {\bm{\gamma}}_{t} adapts the channel filter per query token to drive the bulk of the six-task improvement and surpass Transformer on DROP.

![Image 7: Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376v1/figs/fig7_longctx.png)

Figure 7: Muir extrapolates to \bm{128\text{K}}-token documents and \bm{10\text{M}}-token streams with the lowest perplexity at a constant \bm{25\text{ MB}} state. (a, c) Zero-shot from 4\text{K} pretraining, Dynamic YaRN Transformer diverges past 8\text{K} and unchunked evaluation exceeds HBM at \geq\!128\text{K}, while Muir (static {\bm{\gamma}}) drifts upward past 32\text{K}; dynamic {\bm{\gamma}}_{t} avoids this static-gain drift and outperforms GDN-2 across 128\text{K} documents and 10\text{M}-token streams (2{,}560\times training length). (b, d) After a 64\text{K} cooldown (0.3\% of pretraining), Transformer improves through 64\text{K} before unchunked evaluation exceeds HBM at \geq\!128\text{K}, static-{\bm{\gamma}} drift vanishes, and all Muir variants improve monotonically below GDN-2 through 10\text{M} tokens (160\times beyond cooldown).

#### 5.2.3 Length Extrapolation

[Figures 7](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F7 "In Depth Horizon and Real-World Extractive Recall. ‣ 5.2.2 Associative Recall ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[8](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ 5.2.3 Length Extrapolation ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") evaluate length extrapolation up to 10\text{M}-token language-modeling streams and 1\text{M}-token passkey retrieval at 1.34\text{B} parameters (full tables in [Section H.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A8.SS3 "H.3 Length Extrapolation ‣ Appendix H Evaluation Protocols and Supplementary Results ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). In zero-shot language modeling (Figure [7 a,c](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ Depth Horizon and Real-World Extractive Recall. ‣ 5.2.2 Associative Recall ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), Dynamic YaRN slows Transformer divergence past 4\text{K}, but perplexity rises past 8\text{K} and unchunked evaluation exceeds HBM at \geq\!128\text{K}; even if chunked, a 10\text{M}-token KV cache needs 2.1\text{ TB} in bfloat16, or 0.32\text{ TB} at 2.5 bits ([Zandieh et al., 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#bib.bib127)), compared to a 25\text{ MB}float32 state across all 24 layers. Whereas EOS-separated packed streams reset discourse context and stay flat (\Delta_{\mathrm{tail}}\approx 0), continuous topical accumulation in 128\text{K} documents and 10\text{M} streams causes Muir (static {\bm{\gamma}}) to drift upward past 32\text{K} (\Delta_{\mathrm{tail}}=+0.017\text{ nats}) because a fixed gain calibrated at 4\text{K} over-weights slow-decaying rows once their energy builds over longer horizons ([Propositions 3.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition 3.4 (Stationary row-energy law). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[3.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem7 "Remark 3.7 (Incompatibility of static gain across retrieval lags). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). Adapting the channel filter per query via dynamic {\bm{\gamma}}_{t} cuts tail drift by 3.6\times (vs. static {\bm{\gamma}}) and achieves the lowest perplexity at every horizon up to 10\text{M} tokens. After the 64\text{K} cooldown (Figure [7 b,d](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ Depth Horizon and Real-World Extractive Recall. ‣ 5.2.2 Associative Recall ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), Transformer improves through 64\text{K} before unchunked evaluation exceeds HBM at \geq\!128\text{K}, whereas recalibrated slow-channel gains eliminate static-{\bm{\gamma}} drift (\Delta_{\mathrm{tail}}=-0.012\text{ nats}) and full Muir outperforms Transformer and GDN-2 across the entire 10\text{M} stream.

![Image 8: Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376v1/figs/fig8_longctx_niah.png)

Figure 8: Static matched-filter readout extends passkey retrieval to \bm{1\text{M}} tokens both zero-shot and after a \bm{64\text{K}} cooldown. S-NIAH-1 accuracy across 10 needle-depth bins and context lengths 1\text{K}\text{--}1\text{M} (256\times pretraining length) at 1.34\text{B} scale under (top) zero-shot evaluation from 4\text{K} pretraining and (bottom) pure-text continual cooldown at 64\text{K}. Cooldown extends Dynamic YaRN Transformer from 8\text{K} to 32\text{K} before early-depth accuracy drops at 64\text{K} and unchunked prefill exceeds HBM at \geq\!128\text{K}, whereas recurrent horizons change little under pure-text cooldown: GDN-2 collapses past 4\text{K}\text{--}8\text{K}, Muir (no {\bm{\gamma}}) fades past 32\text{K}, Muir (dynamic {\bm{\gamma}}_{t}, splitting (r_{w},r_{q})=(128,128) for multi-lag recall) holds through 32\text{K} before tapering at \geq\!64\text{K}, and Muir (static {\bm{\gamma}}, keeping r_{w}=256 for single-lag lookup) maintains near-perfect recall to 1\text{M} tokens in both regimes.

On RULER S-NIAH-1 ([Figure 8](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F8 "In 5.2.3 Length Extrapolation ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), unseen RoPE positions limit zero-shot Transformer past 8\text{K}, and raising the RoPE base during cooldown restores 100\% recall through 32\text{K} before early-depth accuracy drops at 64\text{K} and unchunked prefill exceeds HBM at \geq\!128\text{K}. By contrast, recurrent models have no positional encodings to recalibrate, so pure-text cooldown without synthetic passkeys only refines slow-channel statistics and leaves the retrieval horizons learned at 4\text{K} largely unchanged. Under unweighted readout ({\bm{\gamma}}_{t}={\bm{1}}), heavy write-time erasure overwrites distant needles ([Remark 3.11](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem11 "Remark 3.11 (Mechanism of memory-horizon extension). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")): GDN-2 collapses past 4\text{K}\text{--}8\text{K}, and even with low-rank erase regularization ([Remark 3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem3 "Remark 3.3 (Regularization of oblique erase variance by low-rank projections). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), Muir (no {\bm{\gamma}}) fades on early-depth needles past 32\text{K}. Adding matched-filter readout relaxes this erasure; because S-NIAH-1 queries a single needle at the end of the prompt, a fixed filter suffices while keeping the wider r_{w}=256 write gate, so Muir (static {\bm{\gamma}}) reaches 90.0\% zero-shot and 99.4\% post-cooldown recall at 1\text{M} tokens (256\times pretraining length). Conversely, splitting (r_{w},r_{q})=(128,128) under a fixed rank budget in full Muir trades half of that write rank for the per-token routing that resolves multi-lag recall and prevents 10\text{M}-stream drift ([Figures 6](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F6 "In Short-Context Parametric Knowledge and Context-Dependent Readout. ‣ 5.2.1 Language Modeling and Commonsense Reasoning ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") and[7](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ Depth Horizon and Real-World Extractive Recall. ‣ 5.2.2 Associative Recall ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), retaining near-perfect average passkey recall across 1\text{K}\text{--}32\text{K} before tapering at \geq\!64\text{K}.

#### 5.2.4 Training Throughput and Numerical Stability

##### Training Throughput.

Figure [1 c](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") compares training throughput (\mathrm{p50} of 100-step intervals) on 8 TPU7x chips at a fixed global batch size of B\times T=524{,}288\text{ tokens/step} across sequence lengths T from 2\text{K} to 32\text{K} at 1.34\text{B} scale, where both GDN-2 and Muir use our boundary-pivoted chunkwise kernel (complete throughput, compiled XLA FLOP, and HBM traffic breakdowns are in [Section F.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A6.SS3 "F.3 Training Throughput and Numerical Stability ‣ Appendix F Chunkwise Kernel Analysis ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). Replacing the two dense d\times d gate projections in GDN-2 with the fused low-rank projection allows Muir to widen the FFN by +30.4\% (from d_{\mathrm{ffn}}=5{,}888 to 7{,}680) and add the dynamic readout gain {\bm{\gamma}}_{t} while keeping compiled per-step FLOPs (-1.8\%) and HBM traffic (-0.7\%\text{ to }-\!1.4\%) nearly unchanged relative to GDN-2. Consequently, Muir matches GDN-2 at the 4\text{K} pretraining length (179.4 vs. 179.3\text{ K tokens/s}) and runs +1.0\%\text{--}1.6\% faster at 16\text{K}\text{--}32\text{K}.

At T\leq 8\text{K}, Transformer is faster because {\mathcal{O}}(T^{2}d) causal attention is small and Transformer uses a hand-tuned TPU Pallas Splash-Attention kernel fused with block GEMMs, whereas our pure-JAX chunkwise recurrence incurs XLA kernel-boundary HBM reads and writes between chunk stages. As T increases from 4\text{K} to 32\text{K}, quadratic attention nearly doubles Transformer per-step FLOPs (348\to 652\text{ TFLOP/step/device}) and reduces throughput by 4.2\!\times\!. By contrast, because recurrent per-step FLOPs are invariant to T, both GDN-2 and Muir maintain flat throughput across 2\text{K}\text{--}8\text{K}. At T\geq 16\text{K} (N_{c}\geq 64 chunks per sequence at B\leq 32), deeper per-sequence chunk loops allow XLA to fuse boundary temporaries (cutting HBM traffic by 330\text{--}356\text{ GB/step/device} at 32\text{K} vs. 4\text{K}) and overlap asynchronous HBM transfers with MXU execution across chunks, raising memory-bandwidth utilization and increasing throughput by 48\text{--}53\% at 16\text{K} vs. 8\text{K}. Consequently, Muir crosses over Transformer at T\approx 11\text{K} and trains 1.64\times and 2.74\times faster at 16\text{K} and 32\text{K}.

![Image 9: Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376v1/figs/fig9_kernel_stability.png)

Figure 9: Boundary-pivoted chunkwise execution eliminates decay overflow and maintains well-conditioned forward and backward passes. (a) Against an exact float64 sequential reference, naive unpivoted float32 decay factorization overflows and produces NaN on 25\% of heads, whereas the boundary-pivoted bfloat16 and float32 kernels match the reference to machine precision on both chunk outputs and final state. (b) Across synthetic stress regimes spanning \kappa_{2}({\bm{T}})\in[1,10^{4}], the bfloat16 block-Neumann inverse stays near its precision floor throughout the well-conditioned regime (\kappa_{2}({\bm{T}})<40, shaded), which contains the sampled 100\text{B}-token checkpoints of GDN-2 and Muir (markers show medians). (c) Single-layer backward gradients (T\!=\!128,C\!=\!64,d_{k}\!=\!16) match the float64 reference across all six inputs without log-decay drift.

##### Numerical Stability.

[Figure 9](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F9 "In Training Throughput. ‣ 5.2.4 Training Throughput and Numerical Stability ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") verifies the numerical accuracy and conditioning of the boundary-pivoted chunkwise kernel (full tables in [Section F.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A6.SS3 "F.3 Training Throughput and Numerical Stability ‣ Appendix F Chunkwise Kernel Analysis ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")). In Figure [9 a](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ Training Throughput. ‣ 5.2.4 Training Throughput and Numerical Stability ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), forming {\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}} and {\bm{M}} via the unpivoted split {\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}={\bm{\pi}}_{t}\oslash{\bm{\pi}}_{s} divides keys by prefix decay {\bm{\pi}}_{s}=\exp({\bm{\Lambda}}_{s}) in {\bm{K}}_{\oslash\pi}; on fast-decaying channels (25\% of heads) where {\Lambda}_{s,j}<-\ln(\mathrm{FLT\_MAX})\approx-88.7,{\bm{K}}_{\oslash\pi} overflows float32 to \pm\infty, producing NaN in \hat{\bm{K}}_{\odot\pi}{\bm{K}}_{\oslash\pi}^{\top} even though every relative decay {\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}=\exp({\bm{\Lambda}}_{t}-{\bm{\Lambda}}_{s}) lies in (0,1]. Splitting {\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}={\bm{\pi}}_{t,p}\odot{\bm{\pi}}_{p,s} across each sub-block boundary p keeps both exponential factors in (0,1] without division, and maintaining {\bm{\Lambda}}_{t} and the recurrent state carry {\bm{S}}_{t} in float32 keeps forward errors at the precision floor of the GEMM inputs.

In Figure [9 b](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ Training Throughput. ‣ 5.2.4 Training Throughput and Numerical Stability ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout"), the bfloat16 block-Neumann inverse multiplies 7-bit-mantissa MXU operands across leaf squaring and sub-block merges, so rounding error grows with \kappa_{2}({\bm{T}}) and exceeds 10^{0} once \kappa_{2}({\bm{T}})\gtrsim 10^{3}, whereas sequential float32 forward substitution remains accurate across \kappa_{2}({\bm{T}})\in[1,10^{4}]. In trained models, however, exponential decay {\bm{\pi}}_{t,s} across lags t-s and small cross-step inner products between \ell_{2}-normalized keys damp the strictly lower-triangular entries ({\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}})_{t,s}=\langle\hat{\bm{k}}_{t},{\bm{\pi}}_{t,s}\odot{\bm{k}}_{s}\rangle and keep \kappa_{2}({\bm{T}}) well within the stable regime (\max<40 for both GDN-2 and Muir across 1{,}536 chunk systems from the 166\text{M} checkpoints). Moreover, Muir attains lower \mathrm{p90}\kappa_{2}({\bm{T}}) than GDN-2 (7.7 vs. 11.1) because matched-filter readout lowers erasure ([Proposition 3.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S3.Thmtheorem10 "Proposition 3.10 (Erase-readout substitution). ‣ 3.1 Gate Sensitivity and Readout Dynamics ‣ 3 Channel-Wise Gated Delta Memory Dynamics ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), shrinking \hat{\bm{k}}_{t}={\bm{b}}_{t}\odot{\bm{k}}_{t} and further contracting \|{\bm{L}}_{\mathrm{strict}}\|_{2}.

Finally, reusing {\bm{T}}^{-1} from the forward solve and recomputing the boundary-pivoted decay factors across the merge tree keeps \mathrm{d}\log{\bm{\alpha}} at the same precision floor as the query, key, value, and gate gradients (Figure [9 c](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ Training Throughput. ‣ 5.2.4 Training Throughput and Numerical Stability ‣ 5.2 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")), so pretraining runs without loss spikes or NaN s.

### 5.3 Ablations

[Table 2](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#S5.T2 "In 5.3 Ablations ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout") ablates the gate-rank allocation across all four model scales by comparing our sensitivity-allocated Muir ranks (R_{\mathrm{tot}}=512) against wider output-gate allocations (r_{g}=256) and uniform gate ranks (128 across active gates). We also decouple low-rank gate compression from FFN expansion by varying gate ranks and FFN width independently at 406\text{M} (full control tables are in [Section H.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.08376#A8.SS4 "H.4 Architecture and Gate-Allocation Controls ‣ Appendix H Evaluation Protocols and Supplementary Results ‣ Muir: Linear Attention with Sensitivity-Allocated Gating and Matched-Filter Readout")).

Table 2: Sensitivity-allocated gate ranks and dynamic readout match or outperform wider output-gate and uniform-rank allocations while preserving MXU alignment and FFN width. For each configuration of gate bottleneck ranks and FFN width (shown at d=2{,}048), we report FineWeb-Edu validation token NLL across all four model scales, alongside 1.34\text{B} WikiText (Wiki.) and LAMBADA (LMB.) word-level perplexity, 8-task zero-shot commonsense accuracy (LM-Eval), 6-task Based extractive recall accuracy (Based), and 5-shot TriviaQA accuracy (TQA) after 100\text{B} training tokens.
